ANCIENT ORDER IF HIBEP.NIANS.

Under the auspices of division 429 Warrington of the Ancient Order of Hibernians a very successful lecture was given in St. Alban's School on Sunday night last by the Rev. Father Howley. The lecture, which was on Irish subjects historical and antiquarian, was illustrated with excellent limelight pictures and proved both instructive and entertaining. Specimens of the perfec- tion of early Irish Christian art in book illumination, stone and metal work, also some ailment high crosses and ruined abbeys and shrines were thrown on the screen and most interestingly explained. The lecturer dwelt particularly on the great labour of love of the early Irish priests and monks in making copies of the Gospels and particular views of the famous" Book of Kens "—said to I e the most beautiful illuminated book in the world—gave epecial pleasure to the audience. Having sketched in glowing words and beautiful pictures the glory of the ancient days et learning and piety in Ireland the RA,. lecturer gave a brief epitome of the cruel venal laws, ended the wanton destruction of the monasteries end churches and schools of Ireland under Danish and English invaders and led hia audience to the story of the formation of the Ancient Order of Hibernian,' under Rory Oge Oltloore, in the dare of HenryVlll. and Elizabeth, for the defence and protection of faith and Fatherland. Pictures were shown of the priest, surrounded by a reverent congregation, in the act of offering Mars on the mountain-side or in the lonely valley, with Hibernians posted on points of vantage guarding lest the persecuted people should be surprised by the priest- hnoters that were ever on the watch in these cruel dips. The progress and success of the Ancient Order were graphically described doses to the great revival of the ilibernines in 1196; and Father Howley dwelt upon the necessity at the present day of a living Irish Catholic Order to help " the right that needs assistance, and meet the wrong that needs resistance." The advantages of the Order as a Friendly Society among Irish Catholics all the world over were last dwelt upon, and the hope was expressed that the sew division now established in Warrington might prcsper and succeed. —its members ever mindful towards all men of the motto of the Hiberniana " Friendship, Unity, and True Christian Charity." At intervals during the lecture some spirited Irish songs were tastefully given by the children from the Sacred Heart School, and at the conclusion a very hearty vote of than, a was proposed to the Lecturer by Councillor Toole, thechairman, and seconded by air. D. Currie, President of the Warrington Division. Father Howley in replying wished to convey his own thanks sod the thanks of the meeting to the Rev. Father Whittle who kindly lent his school for the lecture. The meeting concluded with the Catholic hymn" Faith of our Fathers." and all present left highly pleased with the first moat interesting and instructive lecture..

CHRISTMAS MERRY-MAKINGS.

One of the ordinary concomitants of Christmas in the olden times was the Yu'e log, or Block, which was placed on the wide stone heat th amidst great rejoic- ings, and generally fired with a brand saved from last year's log Loud was the mirth if it burned and sparkled bravely throughout the night,for thon it before- tokened a year of happiness ; keen was the sorrow Bit suddenly died out. In northern England, so tang as the log lasted—and it was, in fact, a great tree, burning f. r nights and days—the servants of the household were privileged to have ale at every meal. It is doubtful whether such privilege would be much thought of nowadays. Ale is all very well at the right time, but few of us would care to have it for break- fast, or instead of the popular tea in the afternoon. Times have changed in many ways. A really old-fashioned Christmas is s-ldom observed in this country. Coaching days and snow at Christmas exist only in pictures. Still there are acme customs to which we fondly adhere—and sad will be the der when old England shows an apathetic attitude to such institutions as the Christmas pudding and the mince pie. Few there are who trouble to think wherein lies the value of the Christmas podding. Everyone is agreed that it is wholesome and palatable, and in every way delicious, yet many do not take the troub!e to think why.the pudding is so good. The explanation is simple. It is because the Christmas pudding c tains a large !quantity of Cnrrants. All Confections in which Currants preponderate are savoury and pleasant. How delicious are the mince pies, and the famous North cf England Eccles cake. But above all, the Christmas pudding must; be given pride of place. The more Currants it contains the better the pudding. You can easily earn a reputation as an expert maker of puddings or a. y other sweet dish, if you orly bear in mind the magic plover of Currants, Apart also from their delicious flavour, they are the plessanteet form of household economy, for, as men of science base taught no, Currants possess a great food value, are all nutriment and easily digested. The moral of all this is, to ensure a merry Christmas, see that your pudd.ngs and pies contain a liberal quantity of Currants. You will find it a cheap anal effective way of making all around you happy, and thoroughly enjoy the Chtistmas merry-making As a practical and seasonable help to the housewife on whom depends the real pleasure o f the Christmas party, the following recipes have been ;specially preix red by an eminent chef.

INSURANCE BILL-DOCTORS AGREED.

HOW THE BILL ENDOWS THE PROFESSION.

Apparently the last stage of the organized opposi- tion to the Insurance Bill is b be removed by agreement between Mr. Lloyd Geo-ge and representa- tives of the medical profession. The Lobby Correa- pondmx of The Daily News" understands that the representatives of the doctors, who spent several hours with Mr. Lloyd George on Tuesday evening, have expressed themselves satisfied with the amendments to the Bill then agree.' upan and since tabled by he Chancellor.

Many proles-ional men would repudiate the assumption that the privileges of the professi n are in danger, but they cannot lift their voices inde- pendently of the Medical Association. There are those, however, who have the courage to voice their honest convicticns, and among them is Dr. Forbes- Ross, M.D., F.R.C.S , the ea iaent Harley-Areet surgeon. Politically," said Dr. Forbes-Ross in an interview " I am a Unionist, but Ido not consider this a party mea-ure. I venture to predict that this Bill, within, five years, will be ktr wn as Mr. Lloyd George's Bill for the endowment of the medical pr fessicn.

"Consider thi, After spending from £3,000 to £I,OOO in education and medical training, a large number of medicdl students, on becoming qualified, find themselves =cranded through lack of capital and opportunity. The medical section of the present Insurance Bill will endow tl ene with the chance of their Hors, and it will to a gthat extent limit cut- throat competithn. MEDICAL SALARIES.

" Let me explain. In a town or distriet of 50,000 persons insure I, male or female, this yearly medical fee of Gs. a bead provided under the Bill will yield the sum of 300.000 shillings, or £15,000 yearly, for medical salaries. This will afford the engagement of 13 medical men at sslary of Y_l,ooo a year, 30 at A:500 a year, or 00 mediral men at £550. Under present conditions an h a district is now served by about half that Lumber.

" The average income of the medical profession is £22(1 to £2soa year. Under the appointment of GO each will have the maximum average income, with 30 twice the maximum average. It can hardly be realized by the layman what a godsend this will be to young medical men in the future without sufficient means to buy a practice. 'ln addition to having an assured inccme, he will bare the added bleesiog of a constant flow of cases. He will not have to go through a heart-Intuiting time of sitting at home and waiting for someone to pull his front door bell.

"If he is worth his salt, be will accumulate skill and experience, which he will sell at a remunerative price to those of the public who do not come under the Insurance Bill. This is the ' jam ' he will work for instead of having to struggle for his bread and butter. With £250 assured for rent, rates and taxes and bread and butter he uill be in closer compared with his predecessors." Under these circumstances, added Dr. Forbes-floss, in a short time an able young man would be making £6OO to 5:1,000 a year if he made good use of his opportunities. As years pissed he would find it worth his while to relinquish his insurance practice for the benefit of those who come after him, and to make a name for himself in the higher walk of the profession. "Medical men who oppose this measure are unuatingly acting inimically to every existing and future medical student, and consequently indirectly to the general public. Saint Luke is the titular patron saint of the medical profession. and .Escolapius their titular god in mythology. Moro than likely we'll have to add St. David though if political opponents, as most of us are, it may be polite to whisper the com- pleting 'Lloyd George.'"

iIRISH AFFAIRS. NOTES FOR IRISH READERS. NATIONAL LIBERAL FEDERATION AND HOME RULE.

On the motion of Sir George Toulmin, M.P. the Council of the National Liberal Federation assembled a few days ago at Bath, unani- mously carried a resolution heartily approving of the determination of the Government to introduce next session a Bill "to allow the Irish people to manage purely Irish affairs." The event was foreseen. The terms of the resolution had already been published, and it was eear that it would commend itself to an Association which has pledged itself to play a conspicious part in I the struggle for Irish rights. And the Irish 'in England are not wanting in appreciation of that part nor of the significance of the entire proceeding. The Council of the Federa- tion ',pledges its support to a measure of self-government for Ireland not only because of the belief that the welfare of Ireland demands it, but because it must strengthen the Empire of the British, whose power depends in an increasing degree upon the prosperity and contentment of its component parts. The strength of the Home Rule position in contemporary politics is vividly shewn in the speeches by Sir George Toulmin, who moveJ, and Earl Brassey who seconded the resolution. Sir George rejoiced that now when the people's Parlia- ment was unhampered and unshackled of the dead hand of heredity, the first use of this freedom was to be the concession of Irish national claims, for "Ireland had suffered most and had the greatest claim." In no less generous fashion Earl Brassey, acknow- ledged the obligations of England to Ireland. His sympathy is of no new growth. In seconding the resolution he expressed " the deep convictions of a lifetime" and his hostility to the blindly negative policy of Unionism has suffered no abatement in the passage of five and twentyi'years. THE CRY OF "CATHOLIC INTOLERANCE."

The Unionist cry of Catholic "intolerance" in Ireland is not yielding the expected results. A protestant clergyman, who was one of the speakers at the Bath Conference, bore public witness to the dishonesty and malice of this attack upon people who, although having suffered bitter persecutions for their faith are tolerant, nay more, are generous in their attitude towards their fellow-countrymen of another creed. It had been shewn in the most convincing manner that in what was probably the most advertised cases of "boycott " the hostility of the people was not referable to the creed of the unpopular person, but to his part in a transaction affect- ilig the purchase of land—the understandable' hostility of a community towards a "black-leg." In this connection it may be recalled that Mr. Redmond's challenge to the strife mak-rs some weeks ago has not yet been met. English audiences are still wait- ing to hear a recital of his woes from a victim of a Catholic majority—and they will wait many a long day. Mr. Redmond's speech at Banbury was exceptionally interesting. It is the game of the Unionists to represent Home Rule as being "rushed." Such a brilliant discovery is not Mr. Harold Smith's, though he is curiously fond of uttering such an unfounded charge. Mr. Redmond, speaking on a Liberal platform, points to the indisputable fact that for the discussion of the questions from every point of view, a year, or perhaps two years, remains. "We are'' he said "at the commencement of a great con- troversy on the que3tion of Home Rule." Time and the growth of self-governing institutions within the British Empire are on the side of the friends of Irish liberty. The principle of autonomy has been admitted: controversy can only arise as to its applica- tion in Ireland. Why should the system which has made Canada, Australia and South Africa loyal tend to make Ireland disloyal. The only disloyalty is threatened by Sir Edward Carson—the bold Sir Edward with his seven oaths of allegiance to the Crown already sworn !—and the unrepresentative few who have applauded his unworthy talk. " Ulster " says Lord Willoughby de Broke has declared its determination not to submit. That might be confirmed as a rebellious and anarchical proposal, but when any form of government tried to use its power to tyrannise over the legitimate aspirations of a strong and loyal minority, such resistance was justified. He for one heartily 11 wished the men of Ulster good luck in the 1 campaign they had undertaken. They would have at their back not merely the the moral, but very likely the physical support of every

SOAP TRADE BOMBSHELL.

THE ABSORPTION OF CHOSFIELDS' AND GOSSAGES'.

Brunner, Mond and Co 's Huge Deal.

The remaikable soop trade development by which Messrs. Brunner, Mood and Co„ Ltd, the world- famous chemical manufacturers, of Northwich, absorb the great soap manufacturing concerns of Meyers. Joseph Crosfield and Sous Ltd, Warrington, and Meters. W. Gossage and Sons, Ltd., Widnes, was the one topic cf discussion in commercial circles last week-end. The secret of the amalgamation had been well kept and the announcement published in our last issue naturally created much surprise in Warrington. 'The Manchester Guardian" gives the following explanation of the deal :

'Chemical manufacturers, of course, depend very largely upon soapmakers for trade. They find, how- ever, that the soap industry shoes what is to them a disquieting tendency. S3me of the large firms are constantly absorbing lesser ones and then locking round to see how they can avoid the risk of fluctua- tions in the prices of mateiials (mainly fats and alkalis) which thiy use and at the same time find profitable employment for a larger capital. It may be said, indeed, that there is a tendency in large manu- facturing concerns of all kinds to unite their interet-ts with those of the producers of raw materials or the users of the raw materiala which they themselves produce. A notable instance is that of Sir W. H. Lever, who bought the whole town of Lymni about a year ago in order to secure the salt deposits, and when this had been done disposed of the rest, the I sale taking place about three months since. His com- piny has also gone to the Congo State and elsewhere to acquire sources of supply for raw materials, and, if we remember rightly, oil-seed crushing has been added to its business. It has thus to a large extent become independent of extraneous supplies and secured a greater measure of immunity from inconvenient fluctuations.

===== 0002601_19111202_art0016.txt | included=true | included =====
THE NEW ARRANGEMENT.

These circumstances, of course, have a very direct effect upon two sets of parties—those who have been accustomed to getting orders from the soap trade and those who have to compete with a firm which collets its capital in millions and spares nothing that will ultimately conduce to economy. It is not surprising, therefore, that another move should be made, and we have this in the announcement that Messrs. Brunner, Mond, and Co. have entered into an arrangement which means the immediate absorption of or amalga- mation with Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sons, Warrington, and Messrs. W. Gossage and Bons, Widnes. Both Messrs. Crosfield and Messrs. Gossage are among the largest customers of Brunner, Mond, and Co., and the last-named are doubtless anxious to prevent any development which would deprive them of this trade. The soapmakers also will prob- ably fittcl a great source of strength for purposes of competition in their association wNI a powerful concern like Brunner, Mond, and Co. It should be clearly stated that the soap firms are to undergo no change as far as their relations with the public are concerned. They will retain their present names and their me hole of doing business will be substantially the same. The ultimate control, however, will rest with Messrs. Brunner, Mond, and Co., who will give shares in their own undertaking es the price of their acquisitions. What the price is has not yet been dis- c'osed. It will be necessary, of course, to state it to the shareholders of Messrs. Brunner. Mond, and Co., but if only the other parties to the transaction had to be considered the matter would be private, for they are family concern,. The firm of Joseph Crosfield and Sons was founded over a century ago, and it has been a private limited company since ISM. The subscribed capital is £OOO,OOO, and there are also debentures for 5.290,000. In November, 1900—we have no later return—there was a reserve fund of £330,000, and insurance and other funds amounted to £6,815. These figures are an indication of the value of the concern, but in the circumstances they cannot be regarded as anything more. Messrs Gossage's business, we are informed, was founded some sixty years ego. The firm's trade specialities are well-known, but its finances have always been treated ass private mater.

Both Crcsfielda and G saages were in the soap trade " combination "of 1906 with Lever Brothers and othere.

BRUNNER, MOND, AND CO.'S NEW SHARES The deal will necessitate an increase in the authorised capital of Messrs. Brunner, Mond, and Co. At present this is £3,000,000, but only £2,789,650 has been issued. The latter amount consists of £1,962,870 in ordinary shares of 9.1 and £826,700 in 7 per cent. cumulative preference shares. At an early date a meeting of the shareholders will be held to sanction the issue of the further drams required, which, of course, will be a considerable number. The Company has within the last few days announced an interim dividend at the rate cf 2.1 per cent per annum on the ordinary shares, free of tax. For each a the last three full years it has paid 271 per cent., for two years before that 30 per cent., and for previous years even more. In 1910-11 the net profit was £700,499, in 180040 £7313,410, and in 1908-0 £700,130. In each of these years £150,000 was put to the suspense account. On the Manchester Stock Exchange on Friday ordinary shares were sold at Gd. ex-dividend, and the £lO prefererce shares were quoted at £l6 12s. Gd. to £lO 17s. Gd.

ANTI-SOCIALISM.

The Concluding Lecture. The concluding lecture promoted by the Anti- , Socialist Union seas held on Tuesday evening in the Masonic Rooms, Bold-street, the question being the "Complexities of modern industry and Commerce." The meeting was presided over by Mr. H. J. Westbrook and the lecturer, Mr H. S. Saantoo, dealt with his subject in such a manner as to am n the approbation of the Socialists who were present. Mr. Swanton said in Coaling with a'Oif a of that Ikind involving work and labour the Socialists would think its poor compliment unless he referred to them in some sorts? way. They as opponents to Socialism wished to bring all interested in the subject of eocnomics, the worker and the employer on common ground, and to discard all political prejudice, and above all class prejudice which it was the object of Socialist to incite and foment. The only way to prevent people from becoming en easy prey to the unscrupulous agitator was by educating them on ncientifio lines into the problems of modern industry. 'They had to approach the problems in a spirit of scientific inquiry. Dealing with the history of modern industry, the lecturer said that up to the middle of the eighteenth century there was an industrial era known as the era of cottage craftsmen. That era had many things to recommend it, undoubtedly many people regretted that they had got bejond it, but what was the use of indulging in vain regrets when there was no one prepared to go back to that ers,hecause it would involve a lowering of the standard of life. It later developed into the workshop era when machinery came into play, and from ths year 1700 there began that enormous revolution in arts and crafts which had brought about the condition of affairs they had to-day. At about that time there were a number of men whose labours contributed largely to that revolution namely Beloit ley, Arkwright, Crompton, Cartwright, Wedgwood, Watt and Roebuck. From that period arose our com- mercial supremacy. Many people attributed that supremacy to the flical system introduced in 1840 known as the Free Trade system, but they would observe that the foundstions of onr success were laid before (hit system was introduced and it was Upon those foundations hey owed their supremacy. Among other causes for that supremacy were (I) railway development, (2) application of steam to sea transport, (3) a peaceful country whilst other nations were engaged in war, (1) the invention and use of machinery to industry. Free Trade had nothing to do with the question, He was not areing between Free Trade and Tariff Reform but he wished them to guard against false notions and to put down commercial success or failure to the real and tree cause. In view of the way in which industry bad developed and progressed could they possibly suggest that the producer, the worker in the factory, or field, was entitled to the whole of the product s,hich was the result of their labours. Most about all the rest of the community? They must not teoverlooked,in fact they wereabsolutely essential to modern industry, They were the com- plexities which they could not get rid or, but there were other complexities which they could afford to get rid of, end which were clogs entire wheels of progress. The cause was generally some State monopoly or some stupid interference on the part or its State officials. Socialism if put into operation would he full of those complexities and clogs, the result of that hide-bound officialism and State interfeiunce. Such an idea wes absolutely fatal to any progress. The adoption of the metrio system in commerce and trade had rendered it simple and easier for those countries which has adopted it. For the last ihirty-flve yews, Chambers of Commerce had passed resolutions calling upon the Government to establish the metric system in this country in order to simplify our business methods. Th. Government no far had only pissed some Act granting permission for these, who wanted to use it. Such permission was of little use a hen the Government itself continued along the old lines. They ssw to-day in addition to those factors new influences arising in connection with industry and our position in the industrial world. The develop- ment of the United States, Germany and the Far East altered this position very materially. Another matter which had come rapidly to the front was the propaganda —called the universal strike, which was a proposal to bring cspitalista of the world to their knees. It was not a thing which capitalists were likely to do unless driven into a corner. Undoubtedly they would-be enormous losers by it. They could not bring to an end the present system of private ownership by such methods It would only brine ruin and disaster to the population of these islands. The solution for dealing a ith the undoubted fact that whilst prices of necessaries had risen and wages bad not gone up in proportion, was he suggested, that workers and employers should meet ' on common ground. Some kind or iorlustrial board should be formed in the calculi ta ion of which the workers should be repre sashed, and in which the workers would have full confidence. The 1.1 ard should apply its mind to the consideration of those problems, and make suggestions which the Government could carry out and put into practice. If the solution of those problems was approached in that spirit many of those ierious difficulties would before long pass away, as the mist and darkness of night disappeared before the rising , sun. (Afplars!). . -

Questions being invited, Mr. RANDALLS, who stated he was a Socialist, asked whether the Lecturer thought such a commission of working men and masters would last very long, and had it not been tried before?

Mr. SWANIoN seicli;did not think it had been tried on the lines suggested. Mr C LEs another Socialist asked what the lecturer meant by the noisy demagogue. If it meant the s' reet corner orator, he noticed some of the Dukes had taken to tub thumping. Mr SwANTON : They would come under that category. His description related to those men who claimed a "dime all remedy. Mr LF.d : You stated that prices of commodities had increased and wages had not in proportion, yet on the other hand you contend that the competition between capitalists brought the prices to the lowest point. Where was the consistency of the two sugarcoats ?

Mr Swanton : There is no inconsistency at all. The competition between capitalists caused articles to be put on the market at the lowest prices. One reason he gave for the increase in prices was the enlarged output of gold. The price of gold could not he dropped and the price of commodities must rise to meet it. With regard to the nationalization of railways it was asked why could not the railway. if nationalized in England hen succe4 as they were in Germany. Mr Swanton : It is not generally admitted that they are a succeas on the Continent. They could not tell how much more successful they would have been if they had remained 'n the hands of private interests . Do your wish to impress noon or toe the railways in this country area success from the workers iu Ant of view.

SWANTON : It all depends on what you mean by I success. If they had no standard of comparison bow could they tell. Nothing in the world had ever been a complete success. Human nature being imperfect every- thing must be imperfect. Is it not a fact that the cost of transit is greater in England than abroad? The SECRETARY, Mr. McNair, interposed and said the German railways never undertook collection, ware- hosiery, or delivery. Transit was only between station and station.

Mr. S W NTOS said SS a matter of fart capitalists end shareholders were not opposed to nationalization. He 7 as inclined to look upon the nationalizstion movement as a capitalist movement more than a worket's movement.

The CHAIRMAN at the conclusion proposed a vote of thacks ta the lecturer. Ifs cud he could not for the life of him see 69w the amalgamation of everything would help Om to a better state of things. WOMEN'S SUFFRAGE MEETING AT

CHESTER.

Addresses by Miss Crosfield and Lady Frances Balfour.

A large and sympathetic audio, ce assembled hi the Music Hall, Chester. on Tuesday night to hear Lady Frances Balfour and Mrs. Egert mi Stewart Brown oa Women's Suffrage. _ _

Mss r. fl. Cretinsld, will was in the chair, referred briefly to the strength of the Suffrage movement in Great Britain. Therr own society the N.C.W.S.S. alone numbered 300 branches. The one iu Chester being founded 7 months ago by Miss Waring (organizing secretary for Lancashire and Cheshire). It possessed a most able ma sident in Miss M. Itoyclon (Birken- head) who iset present speaking in America for the Suffrage, andwas fortunate in having fund so energetic a secretary as Miss Adams. Miss Croefield then deszrib.d the various types of objectors one met with especially vnorivt women,and advised those who were faint-hearted to c =fort themselves with learning by heart a list of the great men who had backed and were backing their move- ment ; such as John Stewart Mill, Bismarck, Disraeli, Mazzini, the late Lord Salisbury, Mr. Balfour. Mr. Littleton, Lord Salborne, Mr. Boner Law, Lord Haldane. Sir Edward Grey, Mr. Birrell, Mr. LI ryd George, Mr. Runcimin, Mr. Snowden, Mr. Shackleton, Ac, Lady Balfour, who received an eathuaiastic welcome made a weighty and telling speech. She pointed out that there had bees 20 years of hard work to secure the Married Woman's Act. and that just the same arguments were used then, notably that if a woman was allowed to have her own property, she would at once become an unnatural monster, and desert husband and children. None of these alarmist prophecies have been fulfilled, nor have they in the countries where women now possess the vote. It was this type of mind which called Florence Nightinga`e an immodest woman and Elizabeth Fry unwomanly. " Let us remember that it tcok 40 years to emancipate the slaves, and all progress must expect to meet with great attacks. But the vote will remove the last ha 4ge of inferiority in women." Lady Balfour mentioned that Mrs. Philip Snowden alone had addressed 21.11 meetings in one year, and asked where had thee been such masses of meetings for the manhotd suffrage that has been proposed? (Applause.) Mrs. Stewart Brown in a vigorous speech took a hopeful view of the situatirrn and believed in working fur the amendment advised by Mr Lloyd Ge rge. In speaking of bow the vote would effect women's wages, she quoted what Mr. Lloyd George had himself said at a public meeting, "If women had the vote, Govern- ment would have to pay women the some as men." She dealt in masterly style with Mrs. Humphrey Ward's alarmist theories, and showed that belonging to Boards of Guardians Ac., takes up far more time than merely recording votes at election time. The National Anthem was sung and a collect:on taken and the meeting came to a close after new members had been enrolled.

JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

FABLED DRUIDS. I fancy that the President r f the local Druids and his intimate associates, to say nothing of multitudes tutside the Order, iv, old hare only indignation and contempt for one who asserted that the ancient priestly race whose name the former are proud to bear never existed in fact—that they are wholly mythical, and that such records as we have in relation to the subject are to be regnded as pure fiction. Yet it is precisely what an alleged archeologist declared in my hearing a little while ago. " There never were any Dtuids," said this Sir Oracle, with the dogmatic assurance of conscious infallibility, as though the categorical denial, unsupported as it was by any Fort of evidence, admitted of no apical. It occurs to me that I may possibly h ,ye mentioned the extra- ordinary circumstance in this column at the time— one needs a goo I memory indeed if repetition is to be avoided ; but I was singularly impressed, and in any case do not apologise for this reference. Such people irritate one ; at all events, I find them exceedingly unpleasant, and I trust that my readers generally share my sentiments in this re,pect. They tell us that the story of the great Alfred's burning of the cakes in the neatherd's hut is baseless, and that Cnut's rebuke to his courtiers' ridiculously fulacme adulation is likewise devoid of foundation ; that William Tell himself, to whose memory at least one costly monument has been erected, is an imaginary person- age, and that the legend of his desperate achievement with bow and arrow is merOy of the stuff that dreams are made of; that Wellington did not say at Waterloo" Up Gnatds and at em ! and that when cur soloists say that just before Nelson entered upon the Battle of Trafalgar. " along the line the signal ran,' Engand expects that every man tbis day will do his duty'" they do but aid, however unconsciously, in the perpetuation of another bit of supposed English history for which there is actually no ground whatever ! And so forth and so on. Why, these wretched iconoclasts are now doing their utmest to rob the youngsters of their cherished fai y tales ! What next - and next ! IllS FIRST SERMON. That capital narrative as to the late Mr. Job Swales' initial pulpit effort, reminiscent as it was of rertnin of the " Clogshop Chronicles" and of Peter Mackenzie's favourite bits of autobiography, further reminded me of the na-,re easily-daunted young fellow who selected as the text of his trial sermon the most prominent incident in the meagre scriptural account ,of Zaecheus. After reading the passages, the would-be preacher was suddenly seized with the distressing malady known in some other circumstances as " stage fright," and yet again as" blue funk," and his mind, like that f the youthful Job Swales on the like occasion, for a moment became (as in one of his famous roster songs Chevalier hath it) '' a puffick blank." flowerer, he recovered sufficiently to stammer out : " Zacchens was a little man—so am I ; Zaccheas was up a tree - so am I; Zaccheus came down—so will I. " Suiting the action to the word, be beat as dignified • retreat as possible in such conditions. LAST WEEK'S 'STORY.

'The Whistler tit-bit which " Uncle Caleb " intro- duced into his " Corner "last Saturday brought to my mind one that came my way while reporting an inquest once upon a time. Having administered the oath to an Irish widow who had been called formally to identify the body of her deceased husband, the Coroner asked the woman how old she was. "Oi can't tell yer banner that," said she," sure an' oi lift me age in mild Oireland." '' But you can give us some idea " ? pursued the Coroner " Well," was the reply, " Oi reckon vi do be gittin on for 80. " 06, come now," commented the gallant In3:1 of law " you're not nearly so old as that, I'm sure. What was your husband's age ? Can you tell us that ? That, after all, is the main point." "Ali, yes" responded the dame with alacrity, "he'd be about the same age as myself—sixty-four or loins ! " This in turn some- how recalls an interview Inure had with a centenarian of the semi sex and at least equal volubility. " Any children living ?" I queried among other things. "There's two on 'em—booath lads,' she answered, " ther'saar Shi.drack, be's turned o' 70, an' he'll do reyt ennff nab for owt I know, but I'm a bit bothered , about yon yot.og 'un—l'm sadly 'feared I'll ne'er be able to rear him." "Dear me ! " I interposed. " Pray what age is he? " " He'll be five-an'-sixty the next time he makes up!" As the old lady dis- charged this parting shot, there was more than a suspicion of a twinkle in her eye. Of course, as was well-known in all the region round about, the joke was one she reserved for such occasions, and, if not strictly a rigioal it nerved admirably. ESPERANTO.

As to this, just a 'further word, if only to acknow- ledge the complimentary personal allusion of the self- sect ificing and painstaking local hr n. secretary, Mr. E. a. Burgess. I am sorry if fled him to suppose that I had undertaken it really sericus and extended experi- ment in testing the claims of this easily first of artificial internation, I languages. I camsell imagine how disappointed he would le with my perhaps too premature and certainly very feeble effort, but even

that unpretentious little essay sufficed to show that the resources of an otherwise well-equipped printing office are unequal to the demands of Esperanto. However so sanguine and enthusiastic an admirer and disciple of Edmenoff will doubtless find some compensa- tion in the fact that the recipient of my birthday message has caught the infection, and is already making remarkable progress. However that may be he will concede that I aff-treled him one of those opportunities for propaganda, for which, n 5 I pointed out a fortnight ago, 7, or true Esperantiat is ever on the look-out. That is something, for as to this world-wide movement some quite eminent public teachers may be said to have been in a state of gross darkness and superstition. Take a simple illustration such as might be multiplied almost indefinitely. The other week that broad-minded and generally commend- able ecclesiastic Dean Welldon, addressing a gather- ing rf students in their teens, took occasion to warn them against the aitificially-constructed language, mentioning in particular, and denouncing with unusual severity, Volaptik and Esperanto. Now that struck me as a gratuitous exhibition of downright ignorance, and I should not have thought the excellent Dean capable of anything so pitiable. He and I are old enough to remember all about Volapuk (a colleague , of mine studied that system, and gave it fair trial— weighed it in the balance and found it wanting), and the Dean ought to have been aware that it ignominiously expired before any of the boy-s to whom he was speaking were born. Probably he knew that as well as I know it; it was in b-acketing Esperanto with it and treating the two systems' as if there were no essential difference that igno-ance as unwented as it was inexcusable was manifested. As last week's correvondent wrote, anyone who knows anything at all of Esperanto and artific'al languages " Knows that it is a question of Esperanto first and the rest nowhere." A bumble hawker of fish who includes my house in his daily round regularly corresponds in Esperanto with people the world over who krow no more of English than he knows of their native Russian, Japanese, Spanish, Italian, German, French, and the rest. He has an album containing upwards of 200 picture postcards with messages from these unknown sympathisers. Thus people t.-f all nations and tongues systematically rave a medium of communication but for which there could be no such fellowship and heart-union ; and if Dean Welldon had known this (and really one might have supposed that reports rf the annual Congress at any rate would have come his way) he would not have been betrayed into the grave error which I now 'specially deprecate. WOULD-BE SUICIDE'S COMPLAINT.

What the prisoner Richard Pugh said and attempted at Runcorn as stated in last week's " Examiner " reminded me of a case of the kind that came under my own observation years ago. A tramp womau had attempted suicide in a po'ice cell, and I saw her lying on the ground and the brutal gaoler kicking her. His explanation w.s that he was '' teaching her better than trying to kill herself" while she was in his charge. That occurred considerably over 20 years ago, and recently, while on a pedestrian tour in East Lancashire, I fell in wilh a quarryman on tramp between Burnley and Coins. We walked together for close upon four miles. and he confessed that he bad again and again deserted his wife and had repeatedly , been in prison. I ventured to puts few direct questions, and my strange companion proved to be the brother of the inhuman police officer who had used bin heavily-shod feet upon the poor woman so long before. " But," added he " our Ned were different throo me, you know ; he was a converted character—went to t' chapel arid all that, do you see " ? I said I was glad of that, of course but I should hardly have suspected it, " 06, but it's right," eaid he, " I went to his funeral and got to know all about it from a publican in them parts. I told him I reckoned our Ned had been a good customer of his ; and he said " He used to come here regular for a goodish while after he left t' force ; then he signed teetotal and took to going to t' chapel, and we ne'er , saw no more on him at after that." Well well there was lots of room for improvement.

CIIE,HIRE'S NEW COLLEGE.

Cheshire's only undenominstional training college, which has been built on land bought from the Marquis of Crewe, situated opposite Crewe Hall Park, is now completed, and will be opened shortly after Christmas. It bas cost upwards of £30,000, and has accommodation for seventy women, who will be in a hostel on the grounds, and for fifty men. They will have a two years' course, and it is estimated that sixty teachers will be turned out every year.

THE WORLD'S UNREST.

By Mr. H. Whitham.

The time has now gone by when the student of current libtory may confine himself to the study of history as it is made in his own immediate neighbour- hood or nation. Steam and electricity have brought us close together and whatever may bs the happenings between the two poles, such events surely appear each morning, along with the tea and toast, but not r always with an equal relish. So it is, the general elections come along, and I, 6,000 miles away, know the morning after your election in Warrington that von have returned a Tory to represent you in Westminster,—not nice reading certainly, but then, what of it? The world certainly moves all the same. The mills of the gods grind slowly, but finely and inexorably. A recent writer in the "Examiner" suggested that a few of the old historical truths should be placed before the young readers r f to-day. I thought the idea good, and I was more convinced of its wisdom, I as, tuning to my letter book of 1887, I find I wrote a Dumber of articles for the " Examiner" on the then vexed question of Mining Royalties. Mr. Percy, Miner's Agent if Wigan, to 1 up the same question and published a very full pamphlet dealing with it. To-day I find in my paper a despatch from Loucks], stating that a woman reformer, Mrs Anderson, "has by strenuous effort succeeded in doubling the ray of female chain makers" of Cradley Heath, near Birmingham. Turning again to my letter book I find that in 1887 I wrote a number of letters ,to the "Sunday Chronicle" and ti the Warrington "Examiner, " on behalf of the Cradley Heath chain makers. In one of these letters I had this to say : " Sixty hours for 12s, and a strike to maintain it. The truck system with all its attendant oppor- tunities for barefaced robbery prevails. Debt is forced upon the psor miserable wretches, and the charitable and long suffering employers step in and give help by charging at the rate of 2d. and 3d., interest on each shilling. Employers only give work to those who deal at shops at which they are interested. Ore of the men states : All that a man has to take home of a Saturday night is a bellyful of tears." The great draw back of the Cbainmakers Union was that a majority of the members were young girls and women I hare a picture near me which shows the grandmother blowing the bellows, the son and his wife at the anvil, and the grandchild on the hearth playing amongst the cinders. I went through the streets, at d " pubs " of Warrington carrying dog chains and back chains, stating what the makers had for their work, and what the retailers sold them for. I was chairman and Mr. E. Bunch was secretary of the movement and we sent £25 to the Cradley Heath chain makers. No religious body and no men of means stepped to the front to help these women.

During the month of October California as a whole voted a good majority in favour of wom-in suffrage. I am planned to pay I think I contributed a strong argu- ment in refutation of the Anti's argument, which was that the men would always :see to it that the women received justice. That argument is to be found in Professor Cheney's Illustrated Industrial and Social History of England, and in R. H. Home's "A new spirit of the age " article, " Lord Ashley and Dr. Southwood Smith.

All the above is, in part, the cause of the present unreel, and if the workers ask for just a little recogni- tion they receive a scornful "No." If they strike, the "London Morning Poet " advises that " The time has come for that whiff of grape shot which is the only answer of authority to the c'amour of an undisciplined mob." The "Westminster Gazette "puts the situation quarely. "Capital requires a surplus of labour to meet its emergencies, and the reserve army stands idle at its own expense and to its great demoralisa- tion." What are you going to do about it? H. WIIITLIAM.

639, Broderic-street, San Francisco. October, 1911

Important purchaets of property for the improve- ment of the main streets of Warrington were disclosed at a Local Government Board iequiry held at the Town Hall on Tuesday by Mr. Edgar Dudley, F.S I into the application of the Corporation for sanction to. the borrowing of money for the purpose of street improvements. THE LAND QUESTION.

In Relation to "The Social Problem."

By H. T. Weeks.

There is no doubt but that the solutima of the Social Probltm is largely bound np in the solution of the queetion of the " Veto " of the House of Lords. No one can deny, in the face of the accumulated evidence of centuries, that the House of Lords is the stronghold of Monopoly and Privilege. In Mr. Rees' article in "The Young Liberal" a few weeks ego, "The Social Problem," he said it was necesaary that every Young Liberal should ask himself " Upon what principle am I to base my future political action? " There is one straight road, at any rate, in front of all Young Liberals, and that is to endeavour to secure for industry • fairer share of the results that industry produces. Free Trade or freedom of exchange is one of the vital necessities of industry or labour. Having successfully defended " Free Trade" from the attacks of Tariff Reform, the young Liberal must now press on to complete Cobden's work. I say Cobden's work, for in December, 1838, at the instaoce of Richard Cobden, a petition was adopted by the Manchester Chamber of Commerce which contained the following :—" And to carry out to the fullest extent, both as affects agriculture anti manufacturers, the true and peaceful principles of Free Trade, by removing all existing obstables to the unrestricted employment of industry and capital." These " obstacles" were undoubtedly "the abominable ; restrictions in your parish settlements, and the like," a phrase need in his last public speech. The method he would have used to deal with these " restrictions" is contained in a speech delivered on December 10th, I 1841, in which he says : I hope to see societies formed, calling upon the legislature to revalue the land, and put a taxation upon it in proportion to that of other countries, and in proportion to the wants of the State. Industry is to be freed from all obstacles to its unrestricted employment, and land is to be taxed according to the needs of the State. The annual amount required for the needs of local and imperial government is about £326,000,000 which is largely rained by tame and taxes based on the annual rental value of buildings and land together. Now, the site value of land is due to the presence of population and the necessitous demand of that popula- tion for the use of land, according to the conveniences of its position. flat is to say,the community creates the site value; the owners do nothing. But the value of buildings is due to the industry put into them in the shape of bricks, mortar, timber, fittings. Ac. So that by taxing buildings you are artificially adding to the cost of the prcductions of industry, and are so putting an obstacle in the way of its unrestricted employment. But by taxing the rite value of land alone for government purposes y;.O are restoring to the community part of what the community itself creates. Imagine C3'26 ,gioo,orx p raised out of the site value of land alone, imagine what the 525 peers who own • quarter of Great Britain would have to contribute, and see how we should be making the rich pay their due share, while industry and the poor would be relieved. At one stroke you would have attained, or removed the necessity for, half the reforms we are now striving for. Villages, instead of declining, as now, wculd develop into towns, and hamlets would become villages. Small holdings eonld be available everywhere, and under the Tons Planning Act we should have Garden Suburbs Or Bourneville On every side. Instead of legislating for the children bern and reared in an unwholesome slum, you would have healthy youngsters breathing the fresh air of heaven, and growirg in wisdom and stature, aseimilating as only childhood can the thousand and one gifts which nature in her bouety so liberally bestows. The mere fact of • tax on site values would, by making land easily available cause those who wishel to work the land to remain on the lend. Others in town, diegusterl with the tortur- ing competition, would willingly risk wringing out of

the genet ous si il a healthier livelihood than is to be obtained by precarious employment. Cottages would want building, implements wculd be r«mired, clothing must be had, and FO the demand for them would be itcleated. That individuals can obtain a living out of the soil anyone who has vieited the teem-acre farms of Denmark and the rocky patches of the Norwegian peasants well knows. Could we not make sone it the 24,000,000 worth of butter we import? Cculd we not prodne the f.7,000,00n worth of eggs we import? Could we not rear pigs, and so make the £16,00),(100 worth of pork and bacon that reaches these shores from abroad ? But by increasing the means of employmEnt you raise the standard of wages, and a bight r standard of wages means an increased con- eumption of these necessary articles, and so it is highly probable that, although we produced a great deal of butter, eggs and bacon ourselven, the imports would not be materially lessened, and so our manufac- turers would not suffer. And if they did find a diminution of foreign trade the increase int home con- sumption would quite make up for it. Itai•te your revenue both locally and imperially from a tax on site values, snd you have settled the "unem- ployed" question, and the " minimum " wage would settle itself. With lard available for use, men would stay ou the land, return to the land, competitions in towns would decrease, demands for labour would increase, wages rise, rents w,.uld be lower, MA con- ditions healthier and better. You would undoubtedly bring about that better sad more equable distribution of wealth which all Young Liberals an ardently desire.

RAILWAY UNREST.

MR. J. A. SEDDON SAYS DIRECTORS MUST EAT HUMBLE PIE.

Ndignation at Sentences on Earlestown Men.

The Amalgamated Society of Railway Servants held a public meeting for all grades of railwaymen in the Co-operative Hall, Sankey-street, on Sunday eveniug. Mr. W. Hudson, M.P. (Newcastle), was announced as the principal speaker, lint was enable to attend. Mr. J. A. Seddon (prospective Labour candidate for the Newton Division presided over an excellent attendance. The CFI Al It Nt aa: referred to the prosecutions which had taken place since the recent strike. He criticised in strong terms the seuteneee passed upon the inen with regard to the Eariestown distnrbances. Ile contended that the sentences were out of all proportion. and were each RS could not be allowed to continue without a revolt on the part of labour. He pointed out that the struggle between capital and labour would become more fierce and they would also Dave to fight against the judidal bench of the country. They had got to alter the constitution of the courts of law in order to get lair rights and fair play in this country. He was told on good authority that certain people were undertaking most despicable methods in order to find out as to who would be loyal or disloyal should another struggle take pl•ce. There were limits to human endurance, and such people might get more than they bargained for if they persisted in their inr(Wr'es. (Applause). Reviewing the general situation Mr Seddon said it had been contended that the strike was settled too precipitately, and that the C mmission's report men clouded with certain undefinable proposi- tions which bad led to the men having different con- clusions. The railway men started out with the definite determination to get recognition of their unions,which was the minimum of their demtiods. Their case, as far as wages and bourn werecancerned, was unanswerable. He thought the debate in the House of Commons had somewhat cleared the air, end had brought to light the fact that the railway men did not accept the report without question. Notwithau siding the confosion of the politicians, the vote in the House of Cimmons vrould compel the railway companies and the men's leaders to come together and discuss the final agree- ment. If the railway companies refused, the duty of the Government was clear and unmistakable. They must say to the railway companies : "We will not tolerate another dislocation of the t r Ilia. Either you meet the railway men or we meet them. and we shall control the railways in the interests of the c sentry." (Applause) He believed many of the directors would have to eat humble pie, and they would swallow a good deal before they would give up their gr:p of the railway men in the country. Oct of the most hopeful features of the recent struggle VMS the joint aoti on oaths part of the four railway unions. If amalgama- tion was not within the range of pnactical politics, federation ought to be. (Applause.) Mr. W. TA molt moved the following resolution : s' That this mmt rig of all grades of railwaymen, seeing that the industrial battles of the future must necessarily been a wider basis than hitherto, and that the railway organisation means efficiency an well al numbers, calls on all members of railway unions to rem at once for a federation of the various unions, no that in future all shall fight together as one, thus making an injury to one an affront to all. Also, this meeting is determined to press for the removal of our grievances and to stand by the action of the Joint executives to press for a mooting of the railway officials to have the findings of the Royal COMIIIiSSiOn rightly interpreted." Mr. C. Wozawcnorr, in seconding, criticised the attitude of Mr. H. Smith, M.P., on the railway struggle, particularly with regard to the questions he had addressed to the Prime Minister on the subject of alleged intimidati on on the railways. Ile con- tended that they were not consistent with his declara- tion that there was no clam war in Warrington and that the railway men knew how to behave themselves. If Mr. Smith had really sympathised with the men who were out for right and justice he should have come to their assistance at the hour of crisis, but he was scarce then. (Laughter). Ile (the epeaker) hoped railway workers would remember Mr. Smith's attitude when he asked for their votes and not support a man who was against the democracy of this country. (Applausa). Mr. J. A. Wridiam,, local organising secretary. mid it was time railway workers and the industrial classes generally adopted a atrong attitude with regard to the action of the judges in the different Courts. Only the other day the Judge at Cardiff Assizes said It seemed to him that the Trades Disputes Act had been enacted almost solely to create riotait was outrageous torn men in ho position to make such a statement, but all along the line jodges were giving vent to expressions of that kind. Their vices were naturally coloured by the class to which they belonged. There would have to he an entire change in the judiciary system if the workers were to get justice. (Applause). The industrial upheaval throughout the civilized world,con- tinned the speaker, was due to the tcanomic pressure brought about by the development of the capitalist system. The workers were beginning to ask themselves : Is my life to be one of drudgery and toil only? Only half the children of the workers reached the age of floe years and something like 63 per cent, of the children in the elementary schools were defective. In someof the large schools 80 per cent, of the children were under-fed and badly clad. There were no fewer than 1,000,000 people existing in one- room tenements. It wee because the workers were beginning to realize those things that they were show- ing discontent. They were asking whether it was right that palaces should be built on the one hand and miserable slum, remain. The same factors were at work amongst the railway servants. The response to the declaration of a national strike would not have been no

olisnic.iotio if it had only.bfss s qurtiortof ?ympat!ly.

rirdl

The railway worktra hid talit;aed under ti'arriutsin'g conditions for a considerable time, particularly since the institution of the Conciliation Boards. Train loads had increased to the extent of 25 to 30 per cent., and the amount of stuff bandied, as compared with 1907, showed 20 increase of 2,000,000 tone. At the same time there had been a redaction of hands dealing with that greater volume of traffic. Railway profits bad increased from £13,000,000 to .C 17,000,000 but wages had been gradually going down. Those were the causes of the unanimous response that was made in August and be maintained that until they had satisfactory machinery whereby they could remedy their grievances, the discontent and unrest would continue. (Applause.) lie was glad the railway workers bad realized the abtolute necessity of combining the whole of their uniora if they were to bring about the much-desired emancipa- tion. It was the only method by which the 7 could hold their own against the great combinations of employers. As a result of the upheaval, they had been able to gain certain concessions. The companies wanted to make out that those concessions were spontaneous, hut that contention would carry more weight If they bad not been such a long time in grant- ing them. (Laughter and 'Rielly.) Since the estab- lishment of the Conciliation Bonds, the men bad continually asked for increased pay but the companies had turned round and said "We will not give you one penny illetollle until the whole of the awards have 'spiral" Now they had been forced by the exposure

the wor'ters had br eight abcut and the strength of public opinion to grant a few concessions. lie raisin. Mined, however, that the concessions were quite infinitesimal compared a ith what the companies might Ito grant and with what they would have to grant before very long. (Applause.) When the new scheme was announced, railway men generally felt to a certain extent disappointed and he personally did At the some time when they probed deeply into the schema and compared it with the 1907 scheme, they must admit that there were great improvements. Under the old scheme, two month's notice had to be given before a question could he brought before the Conciliation Boards, and than there were great difficulties in getting the hoards to meet. Under the new scheme only 28 daya were to elapse before they got a reply to their petition. The scope of the Conciliation Boards was also extended. Under the 1907 scheme the boards were only entitled to de.l with creations of hours and wages, but under the new scheme they would be able to deal with all conditions of service. That was a very distiuct advance —(applanse)—for in the past the companies had used the limited scope of the boards to their own advantage The six years for which the awards of the board had formerly to hold good was too long when they con- sidered the changes in economic conditions. Under the new scheme an award or agreement arrived at by the boards would terminato in twelve mmths, for, if an independent chairman was called in, in two yea's. The right which the companies had hither assumed to interpret the awards in their own way was &belle' ely wrong, bat whim tha new scheme the interpretation would rent with the C moil stion Bolide. The fact that the secretary of a trade union would be able to act an the men's secretary on the Conciliation Boards ant negotiate with the representatives a the company in case of disputes arising on the boards was, he claimed, an establishment to some event of the principle at recognition. It did not, however, go far enough. (Hear, heir.) Dealing with what he called the bad points of the scheme, Mr. Williams said its scope was too narrow. The Conciliation Boards ought to deal with practically the whole of the grades in the railway service but, under the scherno as at present drafted, their work was confied to thoso engaged in the manipulation of trallin Another detect was th,t the scheme did not deal effectively with casual men, but only with perm afloat employees. F,tty per cent. of the goods warkers in Liverpool ant Birkenhead were casual men. While there was snob a great army willing to do the work at reduced wages, it constituted a great danger to the permsnent employees. (Near, beer.) The time had come when they should say that when a man had been working for a company - for three or six months ha should be classed as a permanent men. in Liverpool there were men who had been engaged as casual, for twelve or fourteen years, sad they °steer knew when they might be dis- missed. Under the new scheme 25 per cent, of the men affected had to petition before any q testion could be discassed by the Conciliation lhartn. Thot was impracticahle and a distinctly retrogressive step as compared with the old scheme, which only required 10 per cent. Again, if the railway c3mpanies wanted to increase the hours, decrease the pay or alter the c on- ditions Is any way detrimental to the men, 25 per cent. must petition against it. That was radically wrong. The responsibility of petitioning might not ti rant upon the rank ant file and the joint execativerwere demanding that when any proposal of that kind was made it should go to the men's side of th 3 Conciliation Boards for discussion. If they were not prepared to accept the conditions, then they must coma before th 'Conciliation Boards. (Applause.) The men ought also to be taken into consideration in the grouping of grades. The selection of the independent chairman of a Cm- ciliation Board should not be left to the Board of Trade. They had had experience of such chairmen in ths past —(mar, hear)—and the men should in future boa factor in the constitution of the panel from which chairmen would be selected. (Applause). Thu recent Commission laid too much emphasis on qmsti els of discipline and management. That was one of the great dangers of the scheme, for it was proposal to allow the companies to decide what came within the definition of discipline and management. They knew what their interpretation Iced been in the post, and they intended to have no more of it. (Applause). It was the intention of the joint executives to have that swept away entirely. They said all grievances should be dealt with by the Conoiliation Boards. (Applause). The railway companies contended that the safety of the public would be enclangerad f questions of discipline and management were taken out of their hands. He maintained that from its very inception of the aim of ' the A.S.R.S. had been to make railway work as safe as possible. (Applause). They had always advocated the installa.ion of safety appliances. The primary consideration of the railway companies was not the protection of human life, but the protec- tion of dividend. A large number of the accidents which happened to-day were din ta the long hours which railway men had to work. The public had every- thing to gain by giving the unions greater power on questions of management and discipline. (Applause.) If at the present juncture the men showed a strong vote in favour of the withdrawal of labour, that withdrawal would never come about. Such a vote would strengthen the hands of the joint executives in their negotiations. Mr. Williams concluded his speech by advocating an eight hours day and a minimum wage of 30e. per week. Mr. M136134;708, of Manchester, also addressed the meeting. Mr. Etti'"ON, of Preston, made a plea for Parliamen. tary action on the part of railway men. He did not mean the kind of Parliamentary action they took in Warrintiton—(laughter)--or action by either of the orthodox political p•rties. They must ad ,pt the methods of the party to which Mr. Seddon belonged. (Applause.) At the next General Election they in Warrington would probably have no interest at stake, because they had not sufficient common sense to bring out • Labour candidate, (Laugh. er.) It was therefore the bounden duty of individual workers to swarm into the Newton Division and sue that Lord Weimer did not secure the laurels again. P"-New\..-w-v\.,--r\•,--'N"-v-r\o-Nd' CHILDREN'S CORNER, Conductcd by UNCLE CALEB

A USEFUL TREE.

Probably the most useful tree in the world is the cocoanut palm, every portion of which is put to good use. The trunk is used for building houses, for making furniture and farm implements and countless other articles; hollowed out it makes a canoe. Its leaves are used for thatching, the leaf stalks for paddles and fishing lines. The blossom in bud makes preserves and pickles beside serving as a good vegetable. From the pith of the trunk is derived a kind of sago, and from the flowers sugar and vinegar. The ripe cocoanut is a valuable article of diet. The white kernel produces a delicious cream, a good substitute for milk, while the oil is used as lubricant for soap and candle making. It is also applied to counteract the stings of scorpions. The refuse of the oil, or oil cake, is valuable as food for animals and poultry, and as fertilizer for the soil. This is what is made at the Oil Cake Works where there was an explosion in Liverpool last week. From the shell drinking cups, spoons, lamps, bottles, firewood, and even tooth powder are obtained. The husk supplies fibre for mattresses and cushions, brushes and mats, ropes,cables, nets and even the harness for bullocks. The web sustaining the foot-stalk is made into strainers and torches. Surely the palm is the most useful of all the trees, except perhaps the modest olive tree of the East. The tree acts as a conductor in protecting houses from lightning.

YULETIDE GIFTS.

And ignorance so terrible is all outgrown at last ; And now, of c arse, sell never be such simpletons again. For Reginild is over nine and I am over too. —Emma Endicott Marean. THIS WEEK'S STORY. "TOO LATE."

This week's story is about the late King ' Edward and his grandchildren, the sons of our present King. 1 At luncheon at Buckingham Palace one of I the boys excitedly said to the king," Oh, l'grandpapa," when the king interposed with Isome severity, "Little boys should be seen and not heard. Goon with your luncheon and don't talk."

The small prince collapsed. Presently the king said to him, relenting: "Now, you can say what you were going to say." The little prince, with a world of meaning, said: "Too late, grandpapa ;it is too late." The king said: " Nonsense ! If it was worth saying five minutes ago it is worth saying now."

- " No, grandpapa," said the little prince. "There was a big green worm in your salad and you've eaten him."

NOTES BY " OVALIST."

Warrington bad ODO of the biggest frights of th:. season on Saturday against Ebbw Vale, and for half the game they were outplayed and out-pointed in • fashion which caused a good deal of detisive cheering from the crowd. Luckily, a change came over the seem in the second half, but when Ebbw Vale got a lead of 10 points to 2. the prospect looked as black as it cou!d do for the Wirepullers.

The cause of all the trruble was the unaccountable change at half back, which the Committee ventured upon at the last minute in the hope of strengthening the three-quarter line. If Lloyd had been displaying 'awe than ordinary good form, I could understand the promotion, but after the display he gave against Wigan Bighfield, only the week before, it was a double surprise ti find him promoted to the first team, and Brooks moved to centre. The result was even more disastrous than I imagined. Lloyd was so slow in getting the ball away from the pack that Creevey and the centres were practically reduced to impotency and the Vale bad no concerted attack to cope with at all. Naturally they revelled in the situation and the homesters were lucky to have only ,ive points scored against them in a first half wherein the Welshmen were consistently a better team.

--- On changing ends Brooks and Lloyd changed aleces. If they had rot done so Warrington would have lost the match, in the opinion of most people. As it was the first attacking movement resulted in Lloyd being placed in possession with Waywell alongside. Instead of a score accruing to the hornet-re Lloyd bungled the transfer no badly that Llewellyn inter- cepted and raced round the posts from half-way, Dai Davies adding an ea,y goal, and bringing the score to 10-2 against Warrington. The position was desperate, but fortunately the team was able to rise to the occasion. Brooks and Creevey rejuvenated the rear division nith some brilliant half-back play, and Woodburn put on the first try with a gallant effort which testified to his pluck and resolution in going for the line when given a decent opening. Then Waywell followed suit on the other wing, and Thomas landed a difficult goal which made the scores " ten all."

The Manchester United have signed on A. C. Saville, a the Lymm Amateurs, an a 'goalkeeper. Saville is quite a figure on any football field, for although only 19 years of age he is flit. sin. in height, and weighs 1414. Be will make his first appearane) for the ticited tc-day at Old Trafford against the Burnley Reserves.

Hereabouts the Vale lost a forward injured, and afterwatcht it vas all plain sailing for the Wirepullera who showed pace and combination which would have troubled the defer ee of most teams. The halves made openings like clockwork, and 13radshaw obliged with a couple f dashing tries which brought the score to 13-10 in favcur cf the locals. The change in the game after the interval was remarkable, and I should imagine the committee will think twice before parting Brooks and Creevey again. They understand one another thoroughly, and without the doughty, robust "scram" half Creevey seems almost helpless. It his to be admitted that Creevey weds suneone to do the donkey work at the base of the pack, and Brooks is the only mm capable of performing the task properly.

With the exception of Lloyd most of the Warring- ton men did well on Saturday, though Tilley was not so safe as usual and compared unfavourably with the brilliance of Chick Jenkins on the other side. Johnston. t 0,,, was weak in midfield and will need to show a good do! more dash to gain the favour of the crowd. Among the Welshmen, Dai Davies was as tricky as ever (though careful of himself) and Llewellyn got his customary brilliant try at Warrington's expense. In the forwards, Foley, the Welsh International, was always conspicuous with good following up. Arnold and Thomas were the star front rank men for Warrington.

I should have thought it would have been wise to I have given Carter a place in the first teem last Saturday, seeing that the opposition was not formid- able on paper, and that the young threequarter was short of practice. As a result of Saturday's idleness he has to do another turn in the " " team to-day, with the veteran Dickenson in the senior ranks At present Carter is the only possibility for centre, and he will win his spurs if he pays strict attention to training. Another debutant against Salford to-day will be Fearnley, a young f ,rward who played so promisingly for the second team at the close of last season. He displaces J. Bradshaw, who has not shown form above the average.

The team selected to go to Salford to-day is: Tilley, full back ; Waywell, Dickenson, Bradshaw and Wood- burn, tbreequarters ; Brookes and Creevey, halves ; 'Thomas, Arnold, Shugars, Cheaters, Johnston and Fearnley, forwards.

This thirteen should have a good chance against the Reds, who have shown only moderate form so far. and were beaten at home by St.Helens just a fortnight ago. Warrington only need to play the game they did in the second half on Saturday, to finish up with a margin of points.

The " Reds' " officials have made thrre changes from last week, Lannce displacing J. Isherweod at full-back, and Evan Thomas and Charles Rees appear- ing in the forwards for J Bevan and Skeldon. Presentetion at the Poll's Station. Go Friday afternoon of last week et-Police Constable Gilchrist, who recently retired from the Police Force on medical certificates,was presented with an oak chiming clock, suitably icscribed, and a gold brooch for Mrs. Gilchrist.—The Chief Constable, who made the prosentation on behalf of the members of the Force, said they all regretted that PC. Gilchrist should have been obliged to retire on account of his health after completing only 111 years service, a period which did not entitle him to any pension, so that all the Police Authority bad been able to do Was to grant him a gratuity. His c imrades had felt they could not let him go without asking him to accept something as a token of their good feeling towards him, and their good wishes for his future. Conse- quently they °sited bin to receive from them the clock, not 'for its intrinsic value, which was not great, but as a tangible expression of what they felt, and they also desired to induct.) Mrs. Gilchrist, and asked his acceptanca of the gold brooch for her.—ex-P.C. Gilchrist feelingly thanked the whole Force for their presents and their good wishes. The clock, which was suitably inscribed on a silver plate, and also the brooch, were supplied byMr. J. Finlayson, Jeweller, Horsemarket-street.

ST. MARY'S ROMAN CATHOLIC CHURCH.

St. Mary's, Buttermarket-street. Re-opening of the Organ.

_ St Maryle Roman Catholic Church, Buttersnarket- street, has long posse-sod sno of the finest organs is the district, but the resources of the instrumeut have recently been extended by its reconstruction on the most up-to-date lines. Tubular pneumatic action has been applied to all secti nee and the organ gains greatly in tone by the addition of an open diapson atop to the great orgen and an Sft. flute stop to the pedal. There are numerous mechanical accessories to facilitate the effective combination of stops and the instrument is blown by electric motor. The enlarged organ was used fir the first time on Sunday and the occasion was masked by the holding of special services. It was a source of disappoint- ment that the work of rebuilding had not been completed in time for the re-opening, but the instsu.. ment was quite playable for accompaniment purposes. A recital wkich was announced to take place after the evening service had, however, to be postponed. The Church itself has undergone redecoration, and the cleaning and painting have been carried out in the best of taste. There was a large congregation on Sunday morning when High Mass was celebrated. The Mayor and Mayoress (Councillor Dr. Joseph) attended. The celebrant was the Rev. B. klawson who was assisted by the Rev. N. Wilson and the Rev. B. Primavesi. The choir, which consists entirely of male voices, gave a very beautiful and reverent rendering of the music of the Mass by Bieck. Tone and balance were alike good and the precision of the attack and attention to details of expression were evidence of careful training on the part of Mr. J. Eckersley, the o ganist and and cbournaster. The Credo was an exceptionally moving and inspiring piece cf work in which the voices cf the boys were heard at their best. Mr. Walter Wallington pissed the organ with marked taste and skill and Mr. Eckers'ey was an able conductor. The preacher was the Rev. Father Be-le, O.F.M. of Liverpool, who gave an eloquent disceurse oa the subject of praise arid sacrifice, taking for his text the 150th Psalm. lie pointed out that it was an obligation and duty for man to turn to the Source of every blessing and bow down in profoundest adoration and offer praise and thanksgiving to Almighty God. Sentiments of gratitude and love were not to be proved by words alone but by deeds and gifts. The instinct to render sacrifice was divinely implanted in the heart ofman

No Bazaars.

The Vicar of Leigh (Canon Irtan Smith) announces that nearly £B,OOO leas been raised since February, 1999, without any bazaars, towards repairing and altering the parish church to ver, new vestries, a new organ, and the church institute. £6,000 was received from frienda outside the parish. - -

_ _ Social Chasm. Bishop of Liverpool on England's Two Nations.

The Bishop of Liverpool, preaching at Christ Church, Southport, on Sunday, said there was growing up in England a reparation of clears. Some of them who were growing old remember the time when rich and poor lived together iu the mire quarter of the city. The lawyer lived close to his office, the merchant close to his business, the tradesman over bin shop. Rich and poor saw much of one another ; the rich helped and europathised with the poor, and the poor were often some of the very bent friends that the rich bad. Co-day our well-to-do people drifted in one direction, and the poor in another. There was riling op in England two nations—a nation of well-to-do people and a nation of poor people, and between them there was growing, alas ! a wider chasm every day. To-day the well-to do people scarcely knew how their poor neighbours lived. Out of sight, the poor were out of mind. Ile as Bishop appealed to them as English Churchmen and English crtizens to help the Natiorral Church to fulfil its function, and to feed its thousands and tens of thousands of souls. If they did not. God wsrned them last summer whet might happen to our country. There was rising up gradually in every large town a mass of people who feared neither God nor man, who were ready on the slightest provocation to break cut into riot and plunder. These were the people they had not ehepherded. they were practically psgens. If we allowed that state of irrelLlon to grow and spread nothing cou'd save this country from anarchy and rum The New Church.
