The Rev. H. Ogden and the Tripe Man

Atrocities.

At tie Go!borne-street Baptist Church on Sunday evening the Pastor (Bev. 11. Ogden) preached on "The Tripolitan atrocities," taking for his text the words " They that take the sword shall perish by the sword" (Matthew cool., 52). He pointed out that history con- tinually repeated the unchangeable law which leen' Christ laid down in the above words. No one would have dreamed when Napoleon had his feet upon Europe, when be bad laid Prussia waste, that in a few years Prmsetia herself would be at tie gates of Paris. But " they that take the sword shall perish by the sword." In. connection with the Tripolitsa etrocitiee, one Wan Warbled that in face of the terrible cruelties perpetrated, so little had been said and so little had appeared in the Engliela papers on the question. Most of what one bed learned hid come through the channel of Francis Mactiliagh. an experienced aver correspondent. Probably for the first time in his life be handed hack to one of the fighting generals his passport and when a war correspondent did that, they might safely B.llllle that not only was there carnage and bloodshed but the most horrible crime. Macullagh instanced a fearful case in which about 400 , helpless men, women and children were led to a portion ' of the ramp and there indiscriminately slaughtered. To add to the horror of horrors, those 403 were taken to a place vrhere all the offal, refuse and rubbish of the camp was pitched and there, huddled in a heap, all unconscious of their doom, they were fired upon Chose who had the means of judging, told as that the troops and even the olli fere wane so drunk with blood that they reeled and staggered like rren under alcoholic influence. One was led to ask how that originated ? Those who knew the Latin , races best declared that they possessed neither the stability nor the gravity of the Anglo-Storin Colonies, I At the commencement of the war the Italians mingled freely with the Arabs of Tripoli, only to retaliate with the most Ravage butchery upon any evidence of treachery. Whatever might he the right or wrong of the order that every Arab subsequently found carrying arms should be executed, there was enrely on warrant for collecting hordes of women and children and as., found without acme, and then, without any trial, mercy or pity, slaughtering them in cold blood. Deeding with English public opinion, the pneachet said one remembered with gratitude the attitude of their leaders with reference to the booing contest which was to hese taken place in London. ren stand taken by the Iles. F. B. Meyer and the Free Church Council led to the rousing of the indignation not only of London but of these islands end whet would probably have disgraced the annals of London was prevented by the emphatic action of thy leaders of religious thought. One felt justified in declerirg that had the Tripolitan horrors gripped the mina and imagination of the English people es it had appealed ,to the war correspondents, the emphatic voice of 'England and her representations at Rome would harts put an end to those terrible crimes. One could only charitably suppose that Italy had been author of the atrocities because her leaders at the seat of war had lost their balance and their juigment, bug unhappily they had brought on their nation dishonour and disrepute. Such barbarity must bring its own punishment to Italy. Christ's words were still the law of the nations : The, that take the sword shall perish by the sword," and as surely as the mills of God ground slowly, just as surely in the cycle of history would those outrages in Tripoli recoil with fearftd results upon the Italian nation. Nevertheless one thing was clear—no European nation could pursue such practices nornolested end unchallenged. Colnes thee ceased the public mind of England must be aroused. Our leaders must take their place in the front. Oar Government and the Foreign Office must be warned that English opinion could not be thwarted, that the voice of England could not be dumb. and Italy most learn that unless she conducted her warfare with such methods as were acknowledged by civilized nation!, she must no longer count on the friendehip of England, but must be bald that this nation would have no deal- ings with those whose hands were red with murder.

Warrington Photographic Society. Mr. Brown, the president, had the pleasure of welcoming a large audience of members on Tuesday to hear another lecture given under the auspices of the Lancashire and Cheshire Photographic Union. Mr. J. Pilkington, of Side Photographic Society gave his paper on "Toning of Lantern Slides." What Mr. Pilkington does not know about this breach of the photographer's art can hardly be worth calling know- ledge, for he has a record of ten thousand finished pictures, and has another couple of thousand slides now ready to bind and finish. Many of the pictures thrown on the screen were very fine both in subject and in tone, and as the formula employed for obtaining each result was tend out, (and was at the disposal of any member present after the lecture) it made a most instructive as well as enjoyable evening. Such method and precision in working are mt followed by the majority of amateurs, else their results would be more satisfactory to them- selves, and their devotion to the art would be more lasting.—Mr. K. F. Bishop moved and Mr. H. Smith seconded the vote of thanks. After this lecture many members' elides were shown on the screen, chiefly for the benefit of the Secretary, who is anxious to secure copies of all such pictures for the record and survey section. With so many and inch rapid changes occurring in Warrington's topography, this effort becomes increasingly necessary, and its results will be of lasting interest and wa-fulness to the town.

Fiddlers Ferry sailing mon. At the headquartt re, the Ferry Inn, Petiketb, this club held their annual smoking concert, which was well attended by membeis and friends. The Commodore, Mr T Bennett, opened the proceedings with a toast to "The King.' —Mr NV Sutton very humorously proposad the toast of "The Ferry Sailing Club.—Mr Smalley, in reply, said that he had heard that-their neighbours at Widnes were about to form a Yacht Club, and said he thought that by combining with the Ferry Club there might be a strong club formed.—Mr. Lewis, of Widnes, said there might be some arrangements made.—The Commodore proposed a toast to 'The Visitors and Artistes." Mr J\V Taylor responded. Mr W Pemberton wee accompanist.

The only answer that A STUDY IN could have been HOCUS-POCUS. expected was given to a question put to the Attorney-General in the House of Commons on Wednesday by Mr. KELLAWAY. The Member for Bedford wished to know whether theattention of Sir Rurus ISAACS had been called to the action of a London newspaper publishing a letter protesting against sickness insurance for domestic servants professing to be written on behalf of forty-seven servants I at Hamblin's Hotel, Old Brompton, whether he was aware that no such hotel existed, and that the forty-seven servants were purely imaginary, and whether he proposed to call the attention of the Public Prosecutor to the matter. The Attorney-General had, ox course, heard of this impudent imposture,but he did not propose to take any action in regard to it. The newspaper to which refer- ence was made was not named in the question, and need not be named here. As COLERIDGE said in a political poem which made a great commotion in its day, "Letters four do form its name." It was probably hoaxed in the matter of the non-existent New Brompton Hotel, but it has been positively asking for hoaxes. If common moral considerations are set aside in working up a case of one sort or another, hoaxes are likely to be welcomed if there is any chance that they may not be found out. The agitation against the Insurance Bill on the ground that it must be injurious to the domestic servant will continue to be historically interesting when in all other respects it is dead and done for. From one point of view it has been most ingeniously worked. Its exposure has now been so com- plete that it need not be argued against. But it has been la most astonishingly clever piece of advertising for the newspaper by which it was originated. "American smart- ness" becomes a poor phrase when we con- sider what English journalists have done in this instance. A fine broad basis for agitation was laid on quite different lines to begin with, but the CHANCELLCR OF THE EXCHEQUER demolished this in a single short speech. Then, without a moment's delay, the train, as one may say was switched off on to another line. At Bow-street, two days since, a Russian gentleman, charged with assisting the suffragettes in the commission of a breach of the peace, said that the women had simply used him as a torpedo to break through the lines of the police. This is just how the domestic servant has been used. It was determined, if that were possible, to break through the lines of the Insurance Bill, and the domestic servant was used as a torpedo. Nothing has come of it, just 89 nothing more than a charge of assault came out of the case of the unlucky Russian gentleman—nothing, we mean to say, injurious to Mr. LLOYD GEORGE'S measure. But a great deal of

light has been thrown on the eccentricities of the human conscience when it is under the influence of strong political prejudice when it is combined with what the Roman Church is accustomed to speak of as invulnerable ignorance. There was a great deal of surprise when Lady BRASSEY was found to be among those who were in front of the so-called "servants" agitation. But Lady BRAME and two other leading supporters of the movement retired at once when they discovered the

real intention of their coadjutors. A great deal, of money is being spent on opposition -t*.the Insurance Bill in the alleged interest of the domestic servants. It has ibeen Stated that only a thousand pounds had been raised for the purpose of financing the agitation. But this is clearly an untrue account of the facts. There has been extensive and costly. printing. Some thousands of pounds must have been expended in distributing copies of a petition through the post, many, if not all, of the petitions being accompanied by a stamped and addressed postcard. Offices have been opened in places where the population is dense and rents are high. A thousand pounds would have been exhausted in what is conversationally spoken of as "no time." What do all this expenditure and all this wild and excited campaigning mean? Actually nothing less than a campaign against the Bill, and not merely against any section of it. It has come to nothing, but it ; will leave a record and a singularly bad one. Its epitaph has been written by the CHANCELLOR OF THE EXCHEQUER. It is the meanest agitation which has ever disgraced the annals of the Press, and, we may add the most shallow and easily exposed.

The letter in our cones - OUR EVENING pondence columns last Scnous. week criticising the administrative methods in vogue at our evening schools—and particularly the course system—seems to have struck a responsive chord in the breasts of numerous students and ex-students in the town. In another column they voice their grievances both categorically and forcibly and on the principle that where there is smoke there must also be fire, the Warrington Education authorities have a case to answer. We have no wish, at this juncture, to enter into the methods by which I the Department sees fit to run the evening schools, but on the face of things it seems conclusively proved that there is a want of elasticity which tends to drive students away from the institutions and makes difficult and to some extent unprofitable, the work of those who remain faithful to the course of study set out for them. We hope an early opportunity will be taken by someone in authority of presenting the other side of the , case to our readers.

Some sensible remarks COLONEL 1, AIR- on tbequestion of home CLOUGH AND THE defence fell from the TERRITORIALS. lips of Lieut-Colonel Fairclough, the com- mander of the local Territorial force, at the Scottish dinner on Thursday He bluntly stated that" The Territorials now stood between this country and conscription," and urged upon both employers and the Govern- ment, a more sympathetic and practical policy in regard to the re-employment of men who had finished their training. The matter should be beyond argument at this hour-o'-day. We have always contended that with adequate recognition of their needs and sacrifices by the State, the Territorials are capable of supplying all the material requisite for placing the home defenco of the country on the soundest possible footing.' If the volunteers and their present-day successors had not been departmentally starved for so long, there would be no one found to listen to-day to the strident cry for compulsory f3'.TVICe in England. Let the [ country make it "worth his while" for the Territorial to volunteer, and the problem is solved for all time!

The 7th annual reOrt CO-PARTNERSHIP Of the Co-partnership HOUSING Ten ants Housing

PROGRESS. Council which was issued a few days ago, shows how steadily the principles of Co-partnership in Housing have, under the presidency of Mrs. Victor Branford, received popular recognition. Figures are given of progress made in connection with 14 estates, which show that the area of the estates secured is 652 acres, while the estimated number of houses when completed is 6,595. Up to the present, £1,100,000 has been spent on property acquired and houses built at : Ealing, Hampstead (3 estates) Garden City (Letchworth) Harborne (Birmingham) Semi:mks Failings Park (Wolver- hampton) Stoke-on-Treat MancLeater Liverpool, and &eland.

Leicester

Dameotwater

Three new Societies have been registered during the year :—Byfleet Tenants, Bolton Tenants and Berkhamstead Tenants. "The notice," says the report, "that Co-partnership in Housing has received abroad is most encouiaging, and the paper that Mr. Henry Vivian prepared for the International Co-operative Congress at Ham- burg last autumn did much to familiarise our principles on the Continent. His visit to Canada, too, has given an impetus to better housing in the Dominion, and the influence of the campaign he conducted is likely to have results of the greatest value to the Canadian people."

FRAWLEY FOR WARRINGTON.

COLONIAL THREEQUARTER SIGNS ON.

Football enthusiasts will be gratified to learn that the long-standing difficulti,-s between Frawley, the Warrington Club, and the Colonial team managers were ended last night when the famous three-quarter signed an agreement to play for Warringmn at the ccnclusion of the (Monisl tour in the middle of January. In ieturn, the Warrington Club released Frawley to play for the Colonials daring the remainder of their engagements, and his name figures on the selected team to play Huddersfield to-day.

Furniture Dispute.

At a special sitting of the Warrington County Court yesterday, befo-e hls Honour Judge Stanger, K C., Mrs. Alice Parr, Knutsford-road, Warrington, brought a claim against Mr. and Mrs. Woodward, 37, St. Mary's-street for the recovery of bou,ehold furniture belonging to Mrs. Catherine Martha Bancroft, deceased, who died at the latter's residence and whose estate letters of administration were granted to the plaintiff as admini,tratrir. Mr. A. Browne appeared for the plaintiff and Mr. T. S. Steel tr the defendant. The contention of the plaintiff was that the whole of the furniture belcnged to the deceased and that she d:ed intestate. The defence was that the goods did not all belong to the deceased bat soma of them belonged to her deceased husband, whose daughter was the defendant. A counter- claim was put ia for money lent to the deceased, and for nursing and board and lodgings for the deceased. The hearing was adjourned until Thursday next.

* * * Councillor Halliwell having set the minimum wage for Corporation employees rolling onward and upward, the senior member for the same ward, Councillor Wilkinson also proposes to join in the move- ment. He will move on Tuesday that the minimum wage of 245. for all able-bodied workmen over 21 years of age be concreted into a Standing Order of the Council on and after the Ist April next. * The "Lancet " this week calls attention to he licking of stamps as a "retrograde pro- ceeding," and says that any suggestion calculated materially to encourage or increase the habit is opposed to a common sense of cleanliness, let alone what bateriological requirements may teach. . . The adhesive stamp is a sanitary blunder, but it is a busi- ness convenience for which it is hard to suggest an alternative. No persons need lick a stamp if they seriously make up their minds never to do so, but unfortunately the use of stamps has created a habit which, once contracted, is difficult to avoid." We wonder if this is a mild rebuke for Mr. F. E. Smith, and the new profession he has suggested ! *

The gathering of the clans at the Warring- ton Caledonian Association's celebration of St. Andrew's Day was, as usual, a most happy and successful affair. It showed that the Scot is not always that dour, stern indi- vidual, incapable of seeing the humorous side of things, which irreverent °southerners often make him out to be. A feature of the pro- ceedings was the flattering reeeption given to Mr. A. H. Crosfield who evidently occupies a warm place in the regard of local sons 0 Scotia. He has undoubtedly earned thei goodwill by his frequently declared admira tion of Scotland and particularly of its Mecca of the golfing world—St. Andrews. In a bright and very appropriate speech he dwelt, with many a touch of humour and several good stories, upon the associations of St. Andrew's Day and also awakened happy memories in the minds of his hearers by a choice reference to the exquisite beauty and romantic history of the Highlands.

The Rev. F. R. Bell, a Halifax Wesleyan minister, has written to the Prime Minister pointing out the electoral disabilities under which Wesleyan ministers (along with all ministers of religion whose churches adopt an itinerating 'pastorate) are suffering through the present electoral system. Mr. Bell cites his own case as that of a minister who has "travelled" 33 years, and yet during that period has only once had an opportunity of voting at a Parliamentary election. The Prime Minister, through his private secre- tary, has sent the following reply : 'The Prime Minister desires me to thank you for your letter of the 18th inst., and to say that the position of Wes'eyan ministers in the existing state of the elect .ral law, is an excellent example of the need of refGrm outlined by him, and the Government hope by diminishing the period of residential rynalificatku effectively to remedy the ' grievanae under which they at present are suffering.". .

Major-General Bethune, who has fre- quently visited Warrington in connection with the Territorial and Boy Scout move- ments, is one of the most unconventional soldiers in the British Army. He has dis- tinguished himself both in peace and in war, in India, in Africa, and at home.. In South . .

Africa he raised and commanded the irregular corps known officially as Bethune's Horse, but better known and respected by friend 1 and foe alike as " Bethune's Bla'guards." They were a happy-go-luckly lot, with never a care for the morrow, and it was their boast, which was no idle one, that they feared neither man nor deviL Despite the fact that

he has been so long without a right hand, has almost forgotten what it is like to be two-handed, General Bethune is an excep- tionally good horseman. By the irony fate he lost his hand, not in battle but while 'engaged in private theatricals. He was John Wellington Wells of "The Sorcerer," the dealer in magical spells, and by the amazing stupidity of a Simla chemist was supplied with a high explosive instead of the harmless "red fire" he needed for the part. • _ •

• - Many of our readers will doubtless have taken the opportunity this week of seeing that Master of the Cue, John Roberts," in play "at Warrington. The spectator does not need to be an expert billiardist to appreci- ate the remarkable genius of the G.O.M. of the green cloth. For all his 64 years John Roberts is still the most fascinating cuist living. Other men may, and do now, make bigger breaks, but there is a personality and magnetism about the old man which are especially his own. We saw him run up 269 unfirished with delightful ease on Tuesday. and those of the younger generation who have never seen John Roberts will be neglecting a duty if they do not visit the Conservative Assembly Room either this afternoon or evening.

* We are requested by the Chief Constable to warn the townspeople—and particularly the shopkeepers—that a number of base coins are being " uttered " in the town, principally florins and half-crowns. Three very presentable but false florins have already been captured, but the circulating medium remains for the nonce unknown.

• • In our correspondence column Mr. T. M. Broadbent draws attention to the humane method of killing animals for food by using the captive-bolt pistol supplied by the R.S.P.C.A. We hope local butchers will show a practical interest in the matter by abandoning the brutal pole-axe and other weapons of a barbarous antiquity, in favour of the painless pistol. • •

- • - "We Germans are conscious of our peaceful intentions, and we have a right to maintain the honesty of our assertions now that the German Empire has served European peace for forty years. We may claim to be so credited at this moment, now that we have made in the Morocco question a sacrifice for the maintenance of peace such as a strong nation of warlike capacities does not easily endure.—Professor Michael in the "Contem- porary Roview " for December. * * *

His Worship the Mayor is inaugurating his second year of office with the welcome proposition that the Council Chamber at the Totvn Hall be enlarged. No one who has experienced the torrid atmosphere engendered by heated debates in this narrow and uncom- fortable room will have a word of unfavourable criticism to utter. Constitu- tions and tempers alike suffer—to say nothing of the dignity of the corporate body of a County Borough.

St. Helens Cable and Rubber Company Ltd., Warrington. . .

The annual dinner of the employe i 4 of the above company was held on Friday last at the Plus Bell Hotel, Warrington, when about one hundred men sat down to an excellent repast. In the unavoidable absence of the General Manager, Mr. J. H. C. Brooking, the chair was taken by Mr. G. W. Price, works' manager. After dinner a smoking concert was held, a notable feature being that all the items on the programme were rendered by members of the Staff, including Messrs. Ford, Seymour, Watson, Bradbury, &c., together with the Works Glee Party. After • most enj ,yable evening the proceedings were brought to a close with a vote of thanks to the Chairman to which Mr. Price suitably replied, and the singing of the National Anthem accompanied by Mr. Willies, pianist. Such were the condemnatory remarks addressed by I the Chairman (Councillor Henry Roberts) at the War- rington Police Court on Tuesday to John Woosey, of White-street, who along with Eliza Emily Partridge, was charged with stealing furniture to the value of £BO the property of Thomas Partridge, C, White-street, the husband of the female defendant. Mr. A. Browne, who prosecuted said Woosey, who was an ex-railway porter at bank Quay Station, who had secured such an influence over the woman that he , persuaded her to leave Warrington with him. Numerous , quarrels had taken place between the husband and wife in consequence of the attentions of Woosey. On Friday, November 17th, the wife threatened to leave her husband, who warned her that if she did go she must not take anything with her. To which she replied she would take "the lot." The husband thought it was an idle threat, but on his return home at night be found the house In darkness and some of the furniture had been taken away. From inquiries he found that two lorries hnd taken it way, the male defendant helping to load. The furniture had been despatched to Edge Hill Goods Station, at Liverpool. On the 23th of November, Sergeant Stuart arrested the male prisoner outside the goods station. li, his possession was an inventory of the furniture. Later, the arrest of Mrs. Partridge was effected. Since the case was adjourned last week, Mr. Partridge had been so upset at his little son crying out for his mother that he had had decided to take his wife back again to live with him. He proposed to offer no evidence against her, and thought that the prisoner Woosey won the sole cause of the trouble. The wife regretted what she had done, and was quite willing to go back borne. The furniture had been returned. The prisoner Woosey slid he admitted helping to take the furniture away. They made it up together to go away, and he went under the impression that the furni- nitnre belonged to her, and not to the husband. The Chairman (Mr. Henry Roberts) said the magis- trates were of the opinion that there was not sufficient evidence against the male prisoner to justify sending lairs for trial to the Liverpool Quarter Sessions. They agreed to the withdrsival of the charge against the wife. The Chairman then passed the strictures given above.

A SHOP RKSE 'WHICH FAILED.

At the Borough Police Court on Thursday, an elderly I stranger who gave the name of William Jones was charged under the Prevention of Crimes Act with loitering in Sankey-street with intent to commit a felony. The Chief Constable explained that on Wednesday week the prisoner wan acting in a suspicious manner in Sankey-street. He called at the shop of Messrs. Gilley, wine merchants and made a purchase of a bottle of port wine. After being served he enticed the young lady assistant outside the shop by asking her numerous ouestiema about some bottles of wine in the window. Whilst thus engaged a confederate of the prisoner went inside and took the cash box. The young lady becoming suspicious returned to the shop and was just in time to see the man about to leave the shop. He at once dropped the cash box end the two men ran away. A young man who had kept the pair under observation followed them for • time but eventually lost sight of them until later in the evening when he saw the prisoner near the Central Station. He informed a police officer of what had happened and the prisoner was taken Into custody. Prisoner put in a plea for leniency and called atten- tion to the fact that the Leavy sentences passed upon him were for crimes when no violence had been used. If he had been tried in the more enlightened days they would not have tees co harsh. He was MI old man and it was time he saw the error of his ways. The Chief Constable said the sentences passed in that Court were far from satisfactory. The Bench sentenced Jones to the full penalty- -12 months bard labour.

UNFIF FOR FOOD.

At the Warrington Police Court on Wednesday. Christopher Horning, 39, School.brow, WU summoned for having in his possession for the preparat ion of sale a sheep's carcass, unfit for human food. Mr. A. T. Hallawvy (deputy Town-clerk) slid that on November 9th Inspector Flood called at the defendant's shop. In one of the rooms used for the making of meat pies and black puddings he found the carcass in a basket covered over with a sack. The carcass was totally devoid of fat, and smelled quits sour.

Dr. Hibbert medical officer of health) said he examined the carcass, which was quite unfit for human food. It was difficult to tell what was the matter with the animal, because the organs were not there for inspection. It was in the early stage of decomposition. Defendant denied that it was for sale. He bought it because he thought they could manage to eat it at home. A fine of El and costs was impaired.

" BRITHER SCOTS."

CALEDONIAN ASSOCIATION'S

ANNUAL DINNER.

Speech by Mr. A. H. Crosfield.

Local Scots, in cc mmon with loyal kinsmen the world rover, gathered round the festive board on Thursday evening to celebrate the anniversary of their patron saint—St. Andrew. No day in the calendar has dearer associations for the sons of Caledonia, and it is always the occasion for renewing old friendships and conjuring up happy recollections of the Land of brown heath and shaggy wood; _ _ . .

„. . Land of the mountain and the-flood. The twenty-third annual dinner of the Warrington Caledonian Association, which took place at the Lion Hotel, was as successful as any of its predecessors, and was characterised by that rich vein of cheery good fellowship which manifests itself wherever Scot meets Scot. There was only one cause for regret and that was the absence,through illness, of the genial President (Mr. J.-Fultra Smith). His place was admirably taken by Mr. J. L. Davenport (vice-president), and amongst others present were : The Mayor (Councillor Dr. Joseph), the Deputy- Mayor (Alderman Forshaw), Mr. A. H. Crosfield, r. W. P. Rylands, Lieutenant-Colonel J. D. Fairclough, Councillors W. 11. BeMan and T. S. Steel, Dra. Naden, Munson, Hutt, Jago, Binns and Burt, Messrs. W. Skinner, W. Silcock, W. 13. Forshaw, G. Bowman, W. Murray, J. Tennant (bon secretary), and B. Wiliamson (assistant secretaty). Evety effort was pot forth to impart the necessary Scottish flavour to the proceed- ings, the guests, each of whom was provided with a sprig of heather.assembling to the stirring skid of the bagpiper,p!ayed, as usual, by Sergeant Donald Murray, of the Liverpool City Police, who wore the traditional national costume. Behind the President's chair were displayed the Scottish national ensign and the blue and white banner a St. Andrew. The menu, of course, included that indispensable Scottish delioacy— haggis—which was brought into the room with the usual ceremonial, the piper beading a proces.ion of waiters and waitresses carrying dishes of the mysterious preparation and a supply of” drams a la Ecossaise." The catering of Mr. John Lee gave every satisfactien. After dinner an interesting toast list was gone through. The loyal toasts having been honoured, the President announced the creeipt of apologies for non-attendance from Sir Gilbert Greeuall, Bart., C.VO., and Mr. Harold Smith, M.P. THE TERRITORIALS AND CONSCRIPTION.

Mr. A. CAMPBELL ROBERTSON proposed "The navy, army and reserve forces," and recounted in glowing terms the vrlorous deeds of the various branches of the service. He said e owed our peace and security to an Invincible navy, and it was comforting to know that at the present time our navy was as near a perfect machine as it possibly could be and that that standard would be maintained. (Applause,) Lieutenant Colonel FATIRCLOI76II responded. He said that man for man, he was tare our army and Territorials would stand up against any foe in the world We must all realize now how very near to war we Were last August and how very little it would have taken to plunge the whole of Europe into a state of conflict. On that occasion there were two of us against one, but 'opposing it had been the other way and there had been two of them against one of us? We had only a small army but he again reprated that it was quite equal to any other army in the world. (Hear, hear.) Rightly or wrongly. we had gone in for recruiting on a voluntary basis and if we could only get the numbers he was sure that was the hest policy to act upon. But at the pres.nt time the Territorials bad not got the requisite numbera and if the full strength was tube ettained,they must have more support from the country. The Territorials gave up a great deal, both 'n time and money—(bear, bear)--and he did not think they Verticuhirly obj-cted to that. What they did want, and what they all looked forward to, was that their places of employment should be kept open for them when they came hack. (Hear, hear.) There were many men who would not join the Territorials because they did not know what would become of their wives and families. Their present employers might be willing to take them back again I but the men said "I don't know where I shall be anyloyed in two or three years and I am not going to I join." 'I he 'Territorials now stood between this country and conscription and unless the numbers could to kept 11. p, be for one failed to see how we were going to have anything else hut compulsion. It all rested with the employment question. If the men could be sure that after they had finished their four years training as Territorials they would get some preference under the Government or in some other way, there would be no few of the Territorial ranks not being Oiled up. !Applause).

"SCOTLAND."

Mr. W. PETER RA LANDS, to whom W3B entrusted the toast of the evening, referred to the appellation 0 bonnie " which was exclusively applied to Scotland. He had spent many delightful holidays in that country and at a suitable time of the year and in a proper place. Scotland might perhaps be called " bonnie." But he was very doubtful whether the Highlands in winter or Glasgow at any time—(laughterl—merited that description. (Laughter.) He believed the word, as applied to Scotland, was invented by a Scotaman—(laughter)—and it was typical of the national character that whatever Scotsmen raid or whatever views they held, they imposed with irresist- ible force on the poor inhabitants of the less important country of England. (Laughter.) It was now taken for granted that because Scotsmen said their country was " bonnie" it must be. (Laughter.) Referring to the extraordinary SIMMS of Scotsmen in tbe world, Mr.Rylands said it was common knowledge that altoost ev ry post of importince in the Gwernment and elsewhere in this country was held by a Scotsman. (Applause.) With regard to the Premiership and other great offices, it had practically become a case of " None but Scotsmen need apply." Mr. Ryland:it humorously commented upon the situation which arose when the leader of the Opposition in the House of Commons—himself a Scotsman—resigned and the diffi- cult question of the appointment of a successor arose. " You have all read a great deal in the newspapers," he said, "onto how that was done. As a matter of fact, what really happened was this : There were two or three men who were obviously likely candidates and when it had to be decided who should have the job,thev all began to turn up their genealogies' trees to see if they were duly qualified. After careful investigation by competent officials, it was discovered that there was only one who was really qualified and he he was promptly appointed to the position. (Laughter.) I have no doubt be will make a most excellent leader 'of the Opposition and possihly afterwards an admirable assistant to his Majesty in carrying on the government of the State." (Laughter and applause). In oonclusi to, Mr. Rylancle said he had great admiration for Scotsmen. He admitted they seemed to get all the beat things in this world, bat he thought they deserved them. Although we poor Englishmen might groan under the domination of the Scotsmen, he felt eure that should the day of trouble ever come we might look with no small amount of confidence to the men from the north country, with their great capaoity both in counsel and in the field, to preserve our country and to maintain the glory of our Empire. (Appirmse).

ROYAL COURT THEATRE.

" The Prince and the Beggar Maid."

Mr. Walter Howa d ranks high as a writer' f.t romantic plays, and "The Prince and the Beggar ' Maid" gives us some of his very best work. It has the combined advantages of a picturesque military and Court telling, a plot full of thrilling interest yet not imp,ssibly sensational, a pretty love story and a genuine humorous element. T i his s not the first visit of the play to Warrington, audit is welcomed with all that enthusiasm which habitues of the Rylands-strset house reserve for special fAvourites. Its popularity is thoroughly well-deserved, for the drama is not merely a clever conception, but its tone is healthy and the action brisk. As presented by Alfred Paumier and Walter Howard's No. 1 company, the interest if the drama never flags for a moment, mull part being well placed. A large share of the honours goes to Mr. Frederick D. Davies who invests the difficult role of Prince Michael, the hump- back, with an intensity of feeling that grips the audience. He is good in every scene but especially in the unexpected climax when he renounces his claim to Monica at the altar. Miss Gladys Lester is a winsome, sympathetic Princess Mon ca and, while fully realizing the emotional possibilities of the various trying situations in allich the finds herself,never shows a tendency to exaggerate. Mr. Henry Ballad is a dashing, impetuous Prit ce Olaf, the hero lover, and makes the most of an attractive Tart. Mr. Alfred Goddard has the somewhat thankless task of impersonating the hectoring, tyrannical P. ince Mildred but he does what is required of him in spirited, realistic style. He is a thorough-paced stage villiar but there is also a restraint, as well as confidence about his acting which makes it very effective. Mr. Royce Carleton makes a cheer y, warrn-hearted Captain Hector and Miss Florrie H. Davies finds much favour as the beggar-maid. Some capital comedy is pro- vided by Mr. Q. Lyon-Hastings as Lieutentant Karl Stromberg and Miss Violette May as Vi la. A striking little study is that of Nathan, the dumb servant, by Mr. Hugh Selwyn. The scenery and effects are most tasteful and elaborate and the trcbestra, under the baton of Mr. T. Boothroyd, plays some pleasing selections.

BREWSTER'S MILLIONS," NEXT WEEK.

--...,- • A good play with a London repatation is always sure of a warm, enthusiastic welcome in Warringion and in " Brewster's Millions," which will be pre- sented at the Ryal Court Theatre next week, we bare a play which cannot be beaten for pmularity in the English Provinces. Miss Emma Hutchison and Mr. Percy Hutchison have during the last 25 years pre- sented upwaids of fifty London successes to their provincial patrons, and " Brewster's Mull,ors " is their greatest success. The plot of the play is very ingenious, and tells how Monty Brewster, who has a fortune of A 250,090, has to spend this large sum of money in one ye a in order to inherit the sum of £2,000,000, but he must not tell any one about it, or get engaged to be married- nor must he waste his money recklessly. He opens an dab rate office, and engages all his friends as managers of the different departments, but instead of getting rid of his money, everything he deals in increases in value. Eventually, he invites them all for a trip round the woild in the steam yacht Flitter, which he has hired because it burns

[SS PHYLLIS STUCKEY as " Peggy.

HENRY HAMPSON as " Monty " Brewster.

more coal than a Dreadnought. This scene alone is worth a visit to the theatre. It cost £2,01-10, and weighs PS tons, and requires fifty stage hands and electricians to work it, and is one of tl.e florist effects of stage management ever achieved. We see the steamer languidly riding at anheor in the starlit dusk of a summer evening just off a Mediterranean poll. The twinkling of tie lights of the town and the phosphorescent glut of the sea are wonderfully real. Then white fleecy clouds befiin to roll across and a storm arises. The yacht is got under way to make a good °filing before it buret. With her engine clanging and a steadily increasing roll, she heads her way to sea. The town disappears and the fiery eye of the lighthouse glides away, the spray begins to fly. the thunder crashes. and the motion of the yacht becomes a staggering, lurching progress through the foam-created billows. It is all so natural that we can literally feel the keen edge of the gale. Mr. Percy Hutchison ~who rras for eihht years with Sir Charlet

Wyndham as stage manager and prodteerl has personally rehearsed this company, and every artiste has been specially chosen for their rtspective parts. He has tees With • " Brewster's lilhioss " fo, nearly three years, and is under contract with Mr celebrated Hutchtaon manage- mem for some time to come. Death of Mrs. J. W. Knowles.

Widespread regret has lymn occasioned by the death of Mrs. J. W. Knowles (wife of Mr. J. W. Knowles, solicitor), who passed away at her residence, The Towers, Middleton, on Tue6clay morning. The deceased lady, who had been in a delicate state of health for some weeks, underwent an operation on the 24th inst , and for a titoe progress& satisfactorily, after which she gradually sank and died as stated. Not only in the village with which she was so closely identified but throughout this district Mrs. Knowles was held in great respect and her death is deeply mourned. The funeral took place at Winwick yester- day afternoon.

DO ELEPHANTS LIE DOWN?

- A question often raised in regard to the African elephant is whether these animals ever rest or not. At li-st blush it would appear that there could only be one answer to the question. So huge an animal must expend a terrific amount of energy, and therefore, an one might reasonably infer, most need more time than smaller animals for rest and reinvigoration. Neverthe- less, many travellers and some naturalists have asserted that the African elephant in its netive state never sleeps, or, at least, never ie down. The correspoadent who has sent us photographs diotct frr m Malek on tbe White Nile stye, in the communication that accom- panies them, that the pictuies represent' A Iterd of female elephants photographed in the bush near Nlalek. in the Moselle Province of the Anglo Egyptian Sudan. It has been meintained that the African elephant never lies down to rest.Sevaral may be seen thus aleeping.'-- " Country Life,"

PICTURES OF THE WEEK.

THE LATE SIR WILLIAM GRANTHAM. Sir William Grantham, better known as Mr. Justice Grantham, died on Thursday afternoon from pneumonia. Always one of the most active of men, it is hard to realize Sir William was in his 77th year and was called to the Bar no less than 48 years ago. He was Conservative Member for East Surrey from 1874 till 1885, sat for Croydon, after the redistribution of seats, until 1886,when he was appointed to the Judgeship of the High Court, which he has held for the long period of 25 years. Sir William was also an accomplished horseman and was often seen, in favourable weather, riding down to the Law Courts from his house in Eaton Square.

A SCHOOL ON A BAND-STAND.

People passing through Regent's Park during the recent cold weather, have been greatly surprised to see a number of delicate-looking children at school on one of the bandstands, where they are exposed to the wind front al quarters. This open-air class has been inaugurated by the Marylebene Dispensary for the prevention of consumption, as most of the children are born of comsuirip•ive parents. The averaga daily attendance is thirty, and the school hours are from 9--5 with a break at 11 o'clock, and another from 12 to 2 o'clock fcr dinner and games. The children are medically examined each week and their temperature is taken morning and event g.

CHESHIRE TERRITORIALS Project for a Comity Range at

Helsby.

At • meeting of the Cheshire Territorial Association on Tuesday it was snnounced that the chief difficulty of the Territorial Force in the county, the lack of a rifle range, wee likely to be solved at last. Colonel Saner esid that after five months' correspondence with the War Office a Royal Engineer officer had been sent down to inspect mike at Ilelsby for a range. Colonel Bromley-Davenport, who wee re-elected chairman of the Association, said financially in Cheshire they were in a very sound position, and no far as their strength was concerned, they had no reason for immediate uneasiness. On November 1, 1010 their strength was 5,130. On November 1 last it was 5,215. The National Reserve movement was proceeding very satisfactorily in the conntry, and before the end of January they hoped the Duke of Westminster would distribute the badges. Discoesing inducements to extension of eervicehe said be was not very fond of the Ides of a bonus—(llear, hear.) It savoured too much of a bribe. But there was something to lie raid for a proposition that an increase of tar might be given, at any rate, to those men who went into camp for fifteen days. If the Government would only devise some- thing which would be a recognition and distinction for the patriotic employer, a great deal of the difficulty would be solved. Colonel Sir Welter Sbakersly vas reelected vice- president.

WARRINGTON CHAPTERS.

HOW THE TIDE WAS TURNED AT OLD ROAD CHAPEL

Not many of our readers are familiar with the Old Road Chapel. but after reading a chapter in its history there may be a few curious ones, who will turn aside to identify it. The Primitiv9 Methodists had their first meeting place here, and the building was in every way worthy of the cause. The secession referred to in the story resulted from the action of one of its ministers, Rev.' George Herod, who removed the backs from the free seats as a means of compelling those who used them to take comfortable seats and pay pew rents. Those who left the Church met in Dallam-lane, afterwards migrating to Buckley-street. Mr. Herod left Warrington during the crisis, and it is his successor, sent by Con- ference to take oversight, who told the

story : I had been stationed three years among a very agreeable, kindly people in a pleasant Yorkshire town, when I was removed to Warrington where I arrived with my wife and family on a lovely morning in August. With some trouble we found the house my predecessor had occupied, and where we were expected to take up our abode. But what a house I What a street! The house we had left had a garden and we could see fields, trees, and hills. Here was a gloomy house which had not been painted for years. Noisy, unwashed children rolled in the gutters, women untidy and dirty sat on door steps and stared at us. My wife stood amazed and suggested that I had made a mistake.

I "No I think lam right" I said for I had heard rumours of the kind of place I was coming to. The tears stood in her eyes as she said "Well, if this is the house what kind of place will the chapel be." "One thing at a time my dear. If you will walk about a short time, I will hunt up the Chapel Steward who will perhaps let us into the house."

I looked into my note book to find the address of the fanctionary who held the high office of Chapel Steward, and after some enquiry knocked at his door. It was opened by a thin-faced woman who to my enquiry called out in a snappish voice." Sammy thee's wanted. Dost hear? Don't keep the man standing." I beard an awkward step on the stairs and soon a man in a leather apron stood before me. He was a shoemaker and a cripple and explained that he would have had the house opened but he was making a pair of shoes for a man who had threatened not to pay unless they were finished that night. Sufficient for one day was the disappoint- ment therof. Next morning we went with many fears to see the chapel. It was as bad as we could have imagined it. Situated in a half-street half-lane it was enclosed in iron railings and had a tumbled down shabby look. The garden or yard in front was completey choked with stones and rubbish, land thistles and grass grew among the ashes the neighbours threw in. The inside of the chapel was little better. The pews—long narrow ones—were dirty and neglected, the clock which was encircled by the suggestive reminder" As time passes eternity approaches," had ceased to mark the march of time and rested from its labours. I noticed on one side of the chapel a row of seats without backs, which I supposed were for the use of the scholars, but on enquiry I found they were the 'free seats" for the I use of those who did not rent the ordinary pews. My predecessor had found that some of the people were willing to sit in the free seats every Sunday and thus escape pew rents. He determined that if they would not pay they should not have any comfort and so he had hacked off the seat backs.

Now came the most disheartening discovery of all. This had caused a secession. The largest part of the congregation had left and set up a kind of religious republic of their own, taking nearly all the children from the Sunday school and leaving only half a dozen adults.

---- --- The next day was Sunday and we saw the full effects of the secession. On arriving we found the door closed. The chapel-keeper had joined the other church and taken the keys with him. After some delay we got inside and old cobbler Sammy dusted a few seats with his blue cotton handkerchief. In all there were thirteen present includ- ing children. One of the congregation had but one eye and a face deeply pitted with small pox. This was Billy Dutton. Then there was Sammy with his iron leg and blue handkerchief. At night I asked al, the members to remain after the service. I found there were only six. Billy Dutton and his wife, Job Smalls and his wife, old Sammy the cobbler and an old nail-maker who seemed to have no name but Dickey. They did not own a pound among them, and here was the crushing fact that the lawyer who held a mortgage upon the chapel had called it in and £lOO and interest must be found at once. I said a few encouraging words, got them to enter into a covenant to pray and work and thus closed my first day's work in Warrington. Next morning I was early at the office of the lawyer who held the mortgage on the chapel. He was a fine-built man, with a shining bald head, grey whiskers, grey bushy eyebrows and a ruddy healthful face. I saw at once he was a man of business. "I have come, I said, to see how much we are in debt to you at the chapel in Old-Road." A look of annoyance crossed his face as he said, "There is a mortgage of £lOO, and interest and costs amount to another £5O. I have been so annoyed I have advised my client to sell it. He has agreed and I have full powers to sell." "Will you allow me three months from to-day to raise the money? I promise if God spares me,,,you shall have the whole of it in that time.

.... The lawyer stood amazed and incredible. I stood bolt upright to let him see I meant what I said. At length he spoke" Well, sir, lam willing to try you, but" (and here the lawyer flashed out) " the money must be paid to the day." I went home and said to my wife "Now my dear you have a silk dress." "Yes, but—" Well I want you to put it on and dress Lucy and Willie in their very best to go to the chapel." "But—"

Aek no questions" I said smiling " When you get there I will tell you what I want. I secured a spade, hoe, and rake and tied them together. When my wife was ready I placed them on my shoulder and we sallied forth in the direction of Old-Road. People

stared just a little, for ministers don't usually carry spades. Arrived at the Chapel garden I set my two children picking up stones and piling them in a corner; to my wife I gave the duty of hoeing up the thistles and weeds, while Ishovelled the rubbish into heaps.

People soon began to stop at the railings and tell each other that "a real lady and gentleman were digging in the chapel yard." Some tried to be funny and brought their friends to help them to laugh,and before dark the whole neighbourhood knew. This was on Monday. On Tuesday morning I received a note written in a bold business hand. Vey. Sir,—l should feel it a favour if yen would allow me to send men to clear away the rub'iish in the chapel yard in Old-road In pa ,sing yesterday I was sorry to see you and your family engaged in so lalLrious a work. A teply by bearer will oblige A FRIEND. I hastily wrote a note of thanks and ' handed it to the man. . .

The next step was to engage joiners, painters and plasterers to repair and beautify the chapel inside and out. Meanwhile I gave each member of our tiny church a memor- andum book and got them canvassing for donations. The faithful half-dozen made splendid sacrifice. Old Sammy entered each week in his book " Soleing and heeling one pair of boots, 3E. 6d." I asked if he could afford this and learned that he got up earlier each morning and managed to do one pair extra. Old Billy made a living by selling tea from door to door and each week "gave a pound of tea to the Lord." Dickey the nail- maker decided to "make every tenth nail for God—a real tithe like those in the Bible." The weeks passed and the chapel was finished. The old clock ticked again in majesty and at the opening services the place was full. Grandest of all, the man who had offered to cart away the rubbish was converted and with a heart full of joy offered a thank-offer- ing of £5O. The three months were now nearly up. We ourselves by the house to house collect- ing and the collections at the opening had got together £9O, so that we only lacked £lO. A friend of mine sent me £5, and now a week from the day of payment we lacked just a little over £5.

The week brought nothing except the small sums our own collectors could put in their books. It was Sunday and I must face the lawyer next morning. The matter seemed hopeless for we had collected from every person in the town who was Wicely to give. I could not rest that night, and so through the long hours we watched and petitioned Him to Whom all the gold and silver belongs. Like a flash it occurred to me to write to the gentleman who had given £5O, and, as a last resource, to ask him to lend £5. I sent my daughter with a note and' waited anxiously for her return. She came back waving a £5 note above her head and crying "Here it is Father, and he has given it to me."

"Given it to you" I exclaimed in choking words. . .

"Oh " she said "When I got to the house he was just coming down the steps. When he saw me you should have seen how anxious he looked. He took the note and tore it open. Then he said 'Thank God it is no worse and gave me this. Then he said ' I was just coming to your house, for I have been troubled in my sleep about your father. I thought he must be very ill or in great sorrow. lam glad it is no worse." An hour later, with head erect and elastic step I entered the lawyer's office. "I have heard of your labours" he said, " and am pleased at your success." I laid down the money and took a receipt. I was leaving the office when the lawyer grasped my hand said "Sir, if ever you want five pounds come to me and you shall have it." Looking at him for a moment I smiled and answere

"Thank you, I will take the five pounds now to help to pay the painters and joiners." He seemed surprised at my sudden claim, but going to his desk brought back a bank note for £5, which I bore in triumph to my faithful friends. .

Thus a new chapter began in the life of Old Road Chapel. and the clock clicked happily on under its ponderous motto through many prosperous years. A. M.

MR. HAROLD SMITH'S QUESTIONS.

The Shops Bill. In the House of Commons on Monday Mr. Harold Smith asked the Secretary of State for the Home Department whether the word occupier in Clause 11 of ' the Shops Bill includes the manager of a shop the busls neon of which is not personally conducted by the employer ; end whether bakers who work on premises where. retell trade or business is carried on will come within the scope of the Shops Bill. The Home Secretary said he was advised that the answer to the first question wise in the negative,and that In such acme the employer was the occupier ofthe shop for the purposes of the clause. As to the second ques- tion, by the definition in Clause 33 of the Bill the term 4 shop assistant " included only such persons as were wholly or mainly employed in a shop in connection with the serving of customers, or the receipt of orders, or the despatch of goods. Persons, therefore, svbo were engaged in the making of bread or other articles would not come within the scope of the Bill.

FROM LABOUR'S STANDPOINT.

By Philip Snowden, M.P. THE AMENDED UNEMPLOYMENT INSURANCE BILL A few weeks ago an explanation was given in this column of the provisions of the Unemployment part of the Insurance Bill. Since then the Bill has gone through a Grand Committee and a number of amendments I have been made, some of which are of import- _ .

_ awe. This Unemployment part of the Bill is not the least valuable, and as it is going to

00-cupations, it is important that it should be carefully considered and understood: . .

It is doubtful if the inducement which is offered by the above quoted provision will be sufficiently attractive to induce employers to keep machinery running at a loss; but it will be a very interesting experiment and time alone can show whether it will be of any value. WORKING THROUGH TRADE UNIONS. An amendment has been made in the part of the Bill which entitles trade unions to work the Unemployment part of the Insur- ance scheme. Only such trade unions as by their rules pay out of work benefit to their members will be allowed to become "approved associations," and only such workmen as prove that they are members of the union shall be allowed to use the union as the medium for obtaining their out of work pay for the national scheme. But the union must undertake to pay one-fourth more to the workman than the seven shillings he ' will get from the Insurance Fund. The members of trade unions which by their rules pay unemployment benefit will be entitled to deduct from their contributions the amount of 21/2d. a week which will be compulsorily taken from their wages by the employer. There is one further point in regard to contributions which may be mentioned as it is an amendment to the original Bill. There is to be no out of work pay for the first week of unemployment. But unemployment for a part of a week only is to count as a week in the matter of paying full con tributions,except that where the period of employment is two days or less, the contributions, both of the employer and of the workman, shall be reduced to one penny if the period does not exceed one day and to two pence if it exceeds two days, and in such cases the reduced con- tributions will only be treated as propor- tionate contributions for the purpose of qualifying for benefits. These are the amendments which have been made in the Bill in Grand Committee, and it is not likely that any material change will be made by the House of Commons.

i'i/si-NCIAL ALTERATIONS.

Alterations have been made both as regards .tbe .s.Dassap.t D 5 DD DI witrir Isms& and the amount of the weekly contributions. In the original form of the Bill a distinction was made between different trades in the matter of weekly benefit, although all paid the same contributions. In some cases it was six shillings and in other cases seven shillings a week. This difference was made because the rate of unemployment in the building trades was higher than in some of the others. But this distinction has now been wiped out, and a sum of 7s. a week will be the uniform rate of out of work pay. It should be mentioned, however, that the Bill does not guarantee the full amount of 79. If the fund should become insolvent it will be in the power of the Board of Trade to reduce the amount to ss. a week, or to order an increased contribution. Apprentice lads will have to be insured, against unemployment. Originally they were required to pay 21/2d. a week like all the rest. This was manifestly hard and unfair, and an alteration has been made to the effect that any workman under 18 years of age shall pay only ld. per week, but his contribu- tion will only be reckoned as two-fifths of a full contribution so that he will have to make five contributions to the ordinary workman's two in order to be entitled to the same scale of benefits. No out of work benefit will be paid to any one during the six months following the commencement of the Act. TRADE UNIONS AND THE BILL. The Trade Union members of the Grand Committee have succeeded in obtaining some modification of the provisions of the Bill dealing with the case of the workman who will not accept" blackleg" employment. It is now clearly laid down in the Bill that a workman shall not be disentitled to unemployment benefit because he -nas refused— (a) an offer of employment in a situation vacant in consequence of a stoppage of work due to a trade dispute. (b) an offer of employment in the district where he was last ordinarily employed at a rate of wage lower, or on conditions less favourable, than those which he habitually obtained in his usual employ- ment in that district, or would have obtained had he continued to be so employed ; or (c) an offer of employment in any other district at a rate of wage lower or on conditions less favourable than those generally observed in such district by agreement between associations of employers and of workmen, or, failing , any such agreement, than those gener- ally recognised in such districts by good employers. I On the other hand a workman is not to be entitled to draw from the fund if his unemployment is due to a strike or lockout. So long as such a stoppage of work exists he

She stood at the window and watched him as he went out of the building and walked, with bowed head and the air of a broken man, along the shabby street. She remained some minutes at the window. buried in thought. She was thinking of Bulmer ; and resolved then to write a letter which he would get on his arrival at the Chase.

With a perfeetly steady hand and complete, composure, she opened one of her trunks and got out writing materials; and making a desk of the trunk, she knelt on the floor and wrote:

"My dear Bulmer,—We shall not meet again. I cannot tell you the reason; but I am forced to leave London and must hide myself somewhere. Do not seek me. That has occurred which must for ever put a barrier between you and me; and the best An well as the kindest thing you can do is to forget that you and I ever met. If you ever learn the reason, you will understand my motives.— Helen."

She read it through two or three times, finding it cold and formal, and wishing with all her heart that she could have put some touch of warmth in it. But that was impos. sible in the light of her new knowledge ; and with a kiss she put it into the envelope. As she was addressing it the thought occurred to her that when Sir Gregory saw her handwriting he would probably open the letter and keep it from Bulmer. She took it out again, therefore, and made a copy. This she encloied in a separate envelope to Sir Gregory, with a note to him.

"I have written a letter to Bn:tiler, of which I send you i copy. I do this in order that you may not think I have broken my promise of secrecy, and so be led to inter- cept it.—Helen Powell." She hesitated some time before signing that name, but to him she could me it; and she was not averse from inflicting the stab which the name would cause.

She did not shed a single tear, nor allow herself to indulge in any regret. She had broken entirely with the past, and must hus- band all her strength to face the future. She accepted the position as unalterable. To look bark was weakness, and of all weakness unavailing regret Was the most useless. As soon as the two letters for tlw Chase were ready, she wrote a short note to Mr. Carwood telling him that certain things had come to her knowledge which made it im- perative for her to leave London at once, and Mat it would be useless tor him to attempt to see her that evening. She thanked him for his kindness, and ended with an assurance that if she could tell him everything he would quite understand her action; and that the persecution which she had endured was at an end.

It is very dis- graceful, I think." As Bulmer read, the frown deepened on his face and his eyes flashed with anger. And with ample cause. 'the newspaper had made a sensational story of the marriage, with half- a-dozen headings in bold type: "A Romance of To-Day. Married in Pocatello. Long and Weary Years. Touching Fidelity Rewarded. A Californian Millionaire Made Happy. His Bride's Struggles in London." And the report gave a graphic account of Helen's life in Massachusetts, where the two had first met; his wooing there, when lie was poor; his sudden wealth; his search for her, and discovery of her in London working as a typist; her engagement to Bulmer; the Cali- fornian's renewed wooing; his sudden recall to the States on business; the awakening of her love for the first suitor; how she had fol- lowed him; and their eventual marriage in Pocatello.

Sir Gregory watched Balmer closely, as he road it through, and waited for him to speak. But he merely folded up the paper. "I will take this, father," was all he said. "Wait; bow did it come to your

"In a wrapper. apparently from the office of the paper, Bulmer. You see the address is typewritten, and the name of the paper is on it."

"I will take this also," was the reply. And putting the two in his pocket, he left the room.

(To be continued.)

There is not a drop of pure blood in the body. until it has passed through the kidneys.

For the kidneys are the blood-filters. From birth to death the blood is con- stantly flowing through them to be filtered. The heart pumps the blood, but the kidneys purify it. They strain out the uric acid poison and waste water, and dispose of it through the urinary system. When the blood reaches the kidneys it is dark and poisonous, when it leaves the kidneys it is the purest blood in the body—all its impurities and waste water have been filtered out.

Every twenty-four hours he k idr eye remcve about 500 grains of urin. u !Jejuna end three Oats of water (run the blood. But when they are weskentd by a co'cl, overwork, or exerse or any kind, they begin to 11. se. their power el separat ng the urine bona the blood.

Cox (chairman of the Solt Unien) appealed to the people of Cheshire and all those interested in the Weaver navigation to put their backs into the question of increasing and improving the navigation. It was a splendid waterway as turns it went, but it did not go far enough. It wanted extend- ing to the Potteries, and even perhaps to Birmingham, and if the County Council and the various councils would really take their courage in their hands he thought they would find it ta their interests to free the navigation of all tolls, and then they would have a magnificent waterway and would be on equal terms with the Continental nations and with America and Canada, where the watersayi were all free. They would then have new industries springing up, and the whole country would be prosperous There were other parts of Cheshire where there were certain to be very great developments. He alluded to Lymm—Sir William Lever's purchase—where it was common knowledge we were on the eve of great developments. There was brine there, and not even the panacea of a three-mile limit would help Mid-Cheshire there, for they had splendid railway facilities to the inland towns, and aove all things they had abundant access to the Manchester Ship Canal. MR. CHARLES LAWS RECITAL-" A CHRISTMAS

CAROL" Parlgate folk had a great treat provided for them on Thursday evening and they did not fail to avail them- selvea of it. There was a crowded audience is the new tVesley Hall to hear Mr. Law, and the utmost apprecia- tion was manifested throughout. Tat of sermons and churches and chapels ! There never was a more beautiful sermon than that of the " Christmas Coral." Snored names do not necessarily mean sacred things. We could have wished that the beautiful and simple language of Dickens bad been trusted by Mr. Law to produce its effect upon his hearers without the adventitious aid of pianoforte accompaniment, whioh in our opinion only marred the pathetic utterances of the characters impersonated. We should have preferred also the descriptive passages to have been delivered in the dry, quiet, conversational style which serves to bring out the full flavour of the Dickensian humour rather than in the somewhat stilted end, at times, almost tragic: monotone adopted by Mr. Law. We think it is a mistake to distrust the effect upoh the audience of word pictures, such as Dickens gives us, and to supple- meat. them by restlem movements about the platform intended to convey the motion that the oharecter is actually before us. The element of intense, melodramatic straining after affect introduced into portion of the narrative by Mr. Law does not, in our opinion, accord with the whimsical nature of the subject matter. For instance, the humour is entirely lost in the description of the leaping up of the dying flame at the entrance of Marley's Ghost which produces a e3rt of chuckle " I know him—Marley's Ghost" by reason of the tragic rendering of it by Mr. Law. Mr. Law's mimetic powers are only in the rudimentary stage of development yet. Dhapite these defects, (as we are bound to consider them in view of such an ambitious attempt.) Mr. Law's recital was a really remarkable achievement. For an hour and a half he kept his audience in a state of thraldom. without a break or interval of any kind—a really wonderful exercise of memory and no slight test of physical endurance. When the conclusion was reached It was with evident regret that the large audience allowed him to withdraw, and he was most vociferously applauded until he returned to receive their renewed acknowledgements. The visit of Scrooge to his nephew's house oq Christmas Day and the scene in Old Joe's Rag Shop, were omitted in Mr. Law's recital and these very fine examples of characterisation were missed with regret by many. The Recital was preceded by a most enjoyable concert consisting of : part song " Killarney " rendered with taste and feeling by Mks S. Silcock, Mrs Cotterill Mr Levi Richardson and Mr M Dutton ; song, "Come into the garden Maud "by Mr Richardson sung with a good clear enunciation but too slowly and with hardly sufficient spirit we thought ; song " Orpheus and his lute" Miss Silcock. The music of this song received most adequate expression, but we could have wished Shakespeare's words to have been heard also, and we failed—or should we ray Miss Silcock failed—in this respect. It was redemanded by the audience, as ales was the part song "The Shamrock " sung :y the same quartet as before—the encore being given without an accompaniment which appeared to be an advantage rethc r than otherwise. Mr. Wilkes capably presided at the piano.

Lanoutt PART V.—At the usual fortnightly meeting of the above on 'Thursday evening of last week, Coun- cillor Forshaw of St. Helens again paid the party a visit, and spuke of the necessity of working class representatives not only being in the House of Commons but also on local bodies. He said that the old idea that all the brains were in the heads of the wealthy had been exploded. In St. Helens their so-called business men had made so many mistakes in connection with Council business that at anvrate the working classes could not do worse. Coun- cillor Forshaw also dealt at considerabie length sir h the work of the Labour Representatives on the St. Helens Council, and instanced the fair wages clause, the minimum wage for Corporation workmen, the reduction of deputation expense!, the feeding of hungry schoolchildren, the alteration of the superannuation scheme for highly paid officials. as matters amongst others which had been fought for in season and out of season by the small band of Labour roeruherr, until eventually they bad been adopted. Labcur repre- sentation in St. Helens bad brought more swietness and justice into the lives of the people, and it could do the same in all localities. As yet,however,they had only just commenced to realise what could be done by having representation from the ranks of the toilers on local governing bodies but he hoped that in all districts they would take the matter or in earnest and use the power of local government to bring more I unsbine and happiness in the people's homer. After a general discussion Councillor Forabaw was warmly thanked for coming that night and speaking to them. Mr Gill was in the chair, a good number were present.

THE LAKE ASD-AVATER,, oRK. At a meeting of the General Purposes Committe held on November oth further correspoidence was read from Sir William Lever as to the proposed purchase of the water works lake and land. After a long discussion it was proposed by Mr Welford and seconded by Mr (lobbed that the committee be anthorised to nob Sir William to give permission to the Council's expert to examine the state of the works and mains and that the Council also obtain Counsel's opinion on the question of the Council's responsi bility if it became the owner of the land including the footpath and the lake and used the lake for revenue purposes. As an amend- ment it was proposed by Mr Holt seconded by the Rev 0 IS Thurston, that before proceeding further the Clerk approach Sir William Lever by letter to see if he was prepared to reduce the price, otherwise the Council could not consider the present offer.—On the amendment beiug put, Messrs Holt, Groves, Davies,

Warburton, Gould, Lockyer, and the Rev 0 B Thurston voted for the same against Median Welford, Gotobed, and Bmith.—On the resolution being put, :Beasts Welford, Gotobed and Sm;th voted for the same against Messrs Holt, Grover Davies, Warburton, Gould, Lockyer, the Rector, the Rev G B Thuraton.—The Chairman declared the amendment carried.

At the meeting on Thursday a letter was read from Sir William in reply to the above correepondence, asking the council to propose a definite offer, and thst after due consideration it was proposed by Mr. Groves, seconded by Mr. Gould, that it be recommended to the council to offer £20,000 for the purchase of the Water Woike lake and land. The resolution was carried, three members, Mears Smith, Welford end Gotobed, abstained from voting.

Tilt Vi' Ale, LANTERN LIN. ITRE.—Contioning the account of his nine weeks' visit to India, the Vicar (Rev. J. Collins) gave another most interesting lecture In the Church Room on Monday evening. Ily chance the Sunday happened to be 'Missionary Sunday, end thin gave the Vicar the opportunity of achieving one of the principal objects for which his Visit to India was made, namely, to see for himself the work that is being done there by the Church. faster than ordinary use. That is, washing in the old way —rubbing and scrubbing. Try this plan—rub the clothes with Fels-Naptha and then let them soak for half an hour in cold or lukewarm water. EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

LORD WOLMER AND THE LABOUR PARTY.

MR. J. A. SEDDON S REPLY. Mr. J. A. Seddon (prospective Labour candidate for the Newton Division) replied at Earlestown on Satur- day night to a recent speech by Lord Wolmer attacking the Labour party. Lord Wolmer had said that the Labour party had pocketed their £4OO salaries, and had also swallowed the insult of having the bill dropped in reference to the Osborne decision. That, raid Mr. Seddon, was not true. The Labour party had refused the bill, and the Government had other work to do. The Labour party were the minority, and if they had fought for all they were worth, they could not have got their bill intro- duced. They did not allow the bill to be dropped, but refused to have it, and there was a lot of difference between the two. For Lord Wohner to any that they were more interested in the £4OO was to make an unwarrantable assertion, proving that he would insult his opponents to make a party point. Lord Wolmer had said they were like tame cats, and that so satisfied were the Labour party with the Libersl Government that they had become part and parcel of that party. Mr. Seddon pointed out that the Labour party strongly opposed the Government on the attitude of the railway companies towards the railway servants. That according to Lord Wolmer, was a sham fight. Lord Wolmer's plea was " Put out the Liberals, and pot us in, and then you will have real fighting men." But they had eomething better than that. 'the Labour party might have made mistakes, but they were the party to decide the best policy, and, if the Labour party did support Government proposals, it was no concern of Lord Wolmer. The Labour party stood as an tndependment party. They were in to get what they could from Liberal or Tory, and as long as they did that, there could be no charge against them that they were not prepared to fight when a labour question came along. (Applause.)

SYMPATHY FOR GERMANY.

LORD NEWTON AND THE RECENT CRISIS.

In the course of • debate on our foreign policy in the House of Lords on Tuesday, Lord Newton (C) said if there was anybody who had drawn any hopeful conclu- sions from these debates he was of a more hopeful and sanguine disposition than he (Lord Newton) was. The outstanding fact sills that in a dispute between France end Germany we bad been compelled to intervene n the part of France. We had, therefore, been instru- mental in preventing the Germans from obtaining what they desired to obtain. That being no and human nature being what it was, there did not seem to be much hope for the belief that the result of these events would cause any better feeling between this country and Germany than had hitherto existed. If an 3 body built hopes on that supposition he was afraid they would be woefully die- appointbd. Everybody must admit that the initial fault rested with the French Government, whose infractions of the limits imposed by the Algeciras Act culminated in the expedition. After that expedition has taken place, German intervention of some kind bad become a certain ty.Then the British Government thought they ought to do something, and Mr. Lloyd George was put up, or pet himself up, to deliver a speech addressed to the German natien. It was a singular fact that this emotional politician, whom so many people would insist in regarding es a statesman, and who had spent the greater part of his political career in denouncing naval and military expenditure, should be the person selected to make a speech which conveyed the impression to the bulk of his fellow-countrymen that we were on the brink of war. Ha could quite understand the German Government cc any government resenting being lectured by Mr. Lloyd George. Surely if a warn- ing were really necessary, the natural and obvious course was that it should be addressei by the Foreign Secretary or the Prime Minister, and in the House of Commons. Such a speech made by a politician of the aggressive type of Mr. Lloyd George was bound to pro- duce a disastrous effect so far es the relations of the two countaies were concerned. If the Government regretted that unnecessary ill-feeling had been aroused between this country and Germany, they had only them- selves to thank for selecting an inflammatory orator of this description to enunciate their policy.
