ST. HELENS.

POINTS SPIKED AT FIAYDOCK.

- As a train, consisting of forty-seven loaded coal waggons and two empty waggons, was proceeding on Saturday in the direction of Oldford Siding, along which is Messrs. R. Evans and Co.'s private line, with the fireman on the front waggon and the brakesman on the buffer of the fifth, the train jumped the metals, several waggons being overturned. The first waggon kept the mils, fortunately for the person who was ' riding on it at the moment, but he received a severe shaking. On examination of the points it was found that they had been spiked open by pieces of stone. and the points further down the line had been treated in a similar manner. The L. and N.-W. signal had been polled down and fixed at " clear," which meant that any train passing was at liberty to proceed. The company have offered a reward of En for the discovery and conviction of the culprit or cutprita guilty of this terrible offence.

LIBERAL PARLIAMENTARY CANDIDATES.

-- Sta.—With the past lisastrous experience of losing two Liberal seats. namely. Oldham and South Somerset, through the mistaken policy of springing utter strangers as candi- dates upon these constituencies against Tories who have lic.en known to the voters for some time, it looks as though the Liberal party are working elections and putting up candidates in a new faugled fashion, which is tending to disgust many sound Liberals like myself. I come in contact with various classes of people in awl around Altrincham Parliamentary Division, both Liberals and Tories, who think there is an awfnl amount of apathy shown since the December election of 1910, and no signs of any Liberal candidate so far. To my mind it will be a great mistake to have any other than a sound business man,' rigidly excluding all lawyers, as too many are already "in the House." It is therefore to be hoped there will be a" waking up" of Liberal officials and a first-class prospective candidate brought before the electors, as who knows when an election may be sprung upon 115. Trusting this matter will lie taken up in the proper quarters and sonic announcement speedily made.—l remain. sir. yours truly, FOREWARNED. Statham, Lyman.

THE SWEDENBORGIANS.

nOtA`it your 1,-.111• 01 Saturday an " Old Journalist " Collllllentti on my reply to his question relating to Swedenborg's re- ference to Louis XIV., George U., David and Paul. 3 cannot say I find his comment quite so fair as his first reference. He Saws that, whether or not the work in which Swedenborg makes the statement he questioned was intended for publication seems to him beside the question. Surely as a journalist he must recognise the difference between a published work, seen through the press by its author, and unpublished memoranda, and see that it is not at all fair to make the latter the criterion and standard of tint cornier, as in the case of Swedenborg numerous writings, your contributor seems to wish, ant/ to wish your readers, to do. To so treat the published writ ings of a man whom he acknowledges to be "entitled to rank among the greatest figures in history," and to be a man " of transparent sincerity and character atsoluteiy irreproachable. HS well as of rare intellectual ability and extraordinary scholarly attainments." especially when that inan of transparent sincerity and irreproach- able character solemnly declares of those pub- lished WlitillgS—" I have never received any- thing appertaining to the doctrines of that Church—Le., the New Church—from any angel, but from the Lord alone, whilst I was reading the Word "—seems to me anything lint fair. I begin to suspect a " red herring," and to doubt whether my courteous interro- gator knows as much of those published writings as I at first credited him with doing. If he did I cannot think he would so cavalierly dismiss the statement of the learned Professor Bush. who certainly did know of what he was speaking. He would also know that the question as to whether the spirits seen by Swendenborg were those of David and Paul, or were impersonating spirits, is a matter of importance, which cannot be dismissed with- out due consideration by the open and candid mind. I do not at all understand what my ques- tioner means by Mr. Claxton and other N.C. ministers being " loth to let 'go the Diary." We do not need to let it go. We simply ask that it may be correctly understood. ap- praised, and correlated to the published works. Your contrihntor may have no use for the "religions system " of the New Church, but at least he will admit he should understand before he dismisses or condemns. I reaffirm, despite his assertion as to his own belief, that no one knows the final state of Paul, and that Swedenborg nowhere states that the Saviour has " everlastingly condemned his faithful servant Paul." Further, let us deal with facts, not speculations. What is there in the " religious system " itself to which your con- tributor objects.? Asking him and your readers in common fairness to ascertain this before they reject what I believe, after long study and investigation, to be truths revealed to the world by the Lord at his Second Coming, and thanking you for enabling me to present the other side of the question.— Believe me, yours sincerely.

W. H. XTON. Great Lever, Bolton. November 28th, 1911

===== 0002601_19111202_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
COES INDUSTRIAL EFFICIENCY BENEFIT THE WORKERS

t1it71e;;;;.‘:)7.7 Labour Omnia Vincit " in your last week's issue comment- ing on my article on Industrial Efficiency lie saya—" I can hardly think Mr. Parker believes that more efficiency means more wages for the worker," and he makes the assertion—a very serimis one if correct—. jf 1 there is one thing the worker dreads it is more efficiency in production, which," he affirms, "means the discharging of men from their employment." This is exactly the ground your correspon- dbnt took up with me two years ago, and it is a pity we should have to retread it. His contention then was, that putting aside the moral and social aspects of intemperance, sobriety amongst the workers would increase unemployment. His words were "instead of temperance reducing unemployment it would increase it," and after quoting Brassey's well known labour experiment on the Great Western Railway, says " that proves clearly then that if teetotalism is adopted we shall have another Li per cent, added to the already 9 per cent, of unemployed." This is logical' if it is true, as he now says that efficiency produces unemployment, then, of course, inefficiency must produce employment. "Labour Oninia Vincit " has therefore made no advance in the two years. I wish I could get him to take a more comprehensive and clearer view of industrial causes and effects. Be sees some industry in which machines are introduced, a certain number of workmen dis- charged. and from this little local back water, this small eddy in the stream of effects flowing from efficiency, he jumps to the con- clusion that efficiency is bad, the working classes should' dread efficiency, and do all they can to make labour less effective. Why cannot "L. 0. V." lift his eyes from the little inlet that fills and limits his vision, and look at the bread flowing tide fraught with good to all? He is like the fish .in this back water, who, could they speak and reason, would affirm that the tide is ebbing because of the direction of the eddy in which they swim. Of course this leaves an opening for our friend to say,..if so lie pleases, that what concerns himself, or some fellow workmen with whom.he is associated. is not the bene- ficent flow of the rising tide, but the effect of the tantalising little back water which has affected them. But this is not the attitude we should expect from one who disinterestedly seeks the betterment of the workers as a whole. All nature is full of sacrifices of the unit for the sake of the whole. The beneficent sunshine at times scorches the grass, the puri- fying wind occasionally levels the sapling as well as the giant oak, but no one would abolish the sun or the wind because their effects are not universally beneficial. And 80 with efficiency, its general effect is to in- crease the reward of labour, but it may disturb labour conditions in a circumscribed area. But I am disposed to think the complaint • about more efficient bibour seriously &solar. log labour is a good deal exaggerated. I

have been engaged in association v itit thousands of workers in industrial develop- ments for the last thirty years, and our con- stant effort has been to increase the inefficiency of labour by the, aid of machinery and in other ways, and I have never had occasion to dismiss any man on account of this increased efficiency. Instead it has resulted in greater demand, and' therefore more employment. If your correspondent will take the figures of employment in those industries in which in- creased efficiency has bad fullest play he will find these are they in which, not only the number of persons employed have increased most, but the wages also.

The fear o. efficiency rests upon the idea that there is a fixed demand for -labour in the world. and a whole superstructure of loo,:e thought is built upon this unwarranted assumption. But we must carry the matter a step further, and if possible make clear by illustration how efficiency creates conditions of well being, and calls into operation labour to produce it. Take the harnessing of the electric current, and the efficiency that has been developed in quite recent years. Tram cars are ono outcome of this efficiency. Here we have quite a group .of new forms of employment capable of absorbing the labour which may have been displaced at points where it had ceased to be efficient. The build. ing of the cars, making of the rails. laying of same, production of electricity, driving of duo cars, the upkeep of the whole are cases in point. For a penny, or a halfpenny, a worker can be carried with comfort and rapidity over a mile to and from his work. Follow this out in a hundred instances and observe the in- creased capacity of money wages to purchase well being. "L. 0. V.“ cannot get beyond the number of coins in his pay box, forgettng that efficiency increased the power of those coins in securing what he desires, and it is in this way that efficiency increases the re- ward of labour. But "L. 0. V." keeps his eyes on the coins, and expects that the result of increased efficiency, say in the work: in which he is engaged, will result in more coins in the pay box. but how can this be so except to a very limited extent? If any depart- ment of industry becomes more efficient the invariable rule (except in the case of a monopoly) is to reduce the selling price of the article with a view to increase the output. This increases employment, butt at the same time it hands over the benefit of the efficiency to the consumer, increasing the real wages of everyone using the article. This is how efficiency increases wages—teal wages. The wage earner then being able to say, say. with ninepence what he formerly bought for one shilling. has a spare threepence to spend in satisfying some new want which, of course, calls forth additional labour to produce the article at threepence. . .

I am aware there are factors which affect exchange values, such as the supply of gold, the variations of nature producing irregu- larities in harvests, &c. These have their influence, but outside these efficiency benefits labour in direct proportion to the saving it effects. The only exception I can think of to this rule is the operation of a monopoly by which the monopolist may he able to garner the result of efficiency, and so withhold it, at any rate partially, from the general body of workers.

CHARLES E. PARKER Penlied). November 28, 1911.

THE EVENING SCHOOLS-MORE CRITICISM.

Snt.—l read with interest the letter front

" A Rejected Student in your last week's issue relating to the organisation of the evening classes, and must say that I am entirely in sympathy with the points he menr tions. It is, I think, about time that the students, mho are the principal supporters of evening classes, and also the persons most concerned in them and their management, bad at least a voice in the matter of subjects. The course system, which compels a student to take a course of study covering three evenings per week at least, may be all right for the younger sttindents entering the schools to enable them to get a good groundwork for the first year or two, but when students enter upon their fourth and fifth years they might at least be allowed to specialise a little in their own particular line, for after all they know best what their individual requirements may be, and not be compelled to take one or two subjects which are useless to them. To quote a case in point :—An acquaintance of mine entered a certain profession; he also took up courses, of study at the evening classes. One of the subjects in the course he took was the same work upon which he was engaged all day long. For the first two years he did not complain, but when he reached the third year's course he asked to be allowed to take another subject in place of the one which, through doing similar work all day, he could practically "do with his eyes shut." This he was not allowed to do, and as a con- sequence when attending this particular class he got through the work in about one quarter of the time allowed, and practically wasted the rest of the evening. The class, from a learning point of view, was absolutely useless to him. He had simply to go to fill in the time. There is a rule in the syllabus that a student wishing to study part of a course may do so with the consent of the principal and the Director of Edncation, and on payment of a sum of Vis. This 1,35. is just double the amount charged for a course of three or four subjects, and for the convenience of studying one out of, say, three subjects he must pay just double the amount he could take the whole three for. Does not this sound absurd on the face of it? And this besides going through numerous interviews and cross. examinations. If only the students were considered a little more, and, as your corre- spondent says, the course system abolished, there would be, I think, less students drop- ping out just at the point they ought to continue, and a great deal more satisfaction to everyone concerned.—! am, Yours, &c.,

J. T. Warrington, November 29th, 1911.

Stn.-1 am glad to see that some one has at last called attention to the farcical course system. " Rejected Student" is not the only one who has met with harsh treatment at the hands of the education authorities. Some time ago I wished to take one sub- ject—i.e., machine construction. In the syllabus it is stated that any student desirous of taking one or more subjects may do so, and when I applied for admission I was told I must take the whole course or not at all. Then I was met with the retort, "We have already got one hundred students to take the full course, and we can do very well without your sort. If you want to make a speech you had better pass on to the next building." Splendid encouragement for an intending student! How can students take a course of three or four subjects besides home work and follow his employment for nine or ten hours every day P—Yours . _

Congratulations are the due of both the Corporation and the Museum Committee on having acquired the above picture by Sir Luke Fildes, R.A., which was exhibited at the Royal Academy in 1872 and went a long wry towards establishing the artist in that great reputation which makes Warrington proud to call him her son. It bteatne known that the picture was to be on sale at Christie's, and Councillor Thomas Birtles was deputed by the Museum Committee, who, with the Town Clerk, are commendably energetic in this particular sphere of action, to attend Christie's saleroom with the object of purchasing the picture. It was a treasure, from a Warrington point of view, in more ways than one, for Luke Fildes is a former student of the Warrington School of Art, and a special effort was warranted to make such a substantial addition to our rapidly-growing collection of pictures by local artists. Councillor Birtles attended the sale in London and was fortunate in being able to secure the picture for the very reasonable sum of 280 guineas. It is worth probably three times as much and only its size (96 inches by 54 inches) and its obvious suitability for a public gallery, kept the bidding very quiet. Owing to the fact that the Museum Committee has no great financial resources, a guarantee to help in acquiring the picture for the town was very kindly given by Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart., and this made the way easy for the purchase on behalf of the Corporation. _

'We have had an opportunity of seeing "Fair Quiet and Sweet Rest" in the studio of Mr. Birtles, prior to its early removal to the Art Gallery in Bold-street, and can 53y at once that it is a fine example of Fildes' work, full of sylvan beauty, soft sweet charm, delicacy, yet warmth of colouring. It should prove a great incentive to Warrington art students, and inspire them with the hope of some day reaching the loftiest heights in a noble calling. Since securing the picture Mr. Birtles has had a letter from Sir Luke Fiides expressing his gratification at the fact that it has been secured for the Warrington Art Gallery at a price which he calls "remarkably cheap." He offers to personally superintend the cleaning of the canvas if required, but, as it happens, the picture has recently been cleaned, and only requires glazing preparatory to being hung on Warrington walls.

ANOTHER 'REJECTED STUDENT. November 30th.

Sir.—Your correspondent " Rejected Student," in your hat issue raises a question of great importance to technical students, and one which is of public interest as affecting industrial efficiency. The "curse ' system under which technical education is organised in some centres—including Warrington - certainly has advantages as applied to the class of young students on the threshold of a busi- ness career, and desirous of following up an elementary training towards the special line in which farther efforts are to be directed. Therefore, should he be specialising in chemistry, he may study that subject at the school on the understanding that classes in mathematics, phy.ics, etc., as the case may be, will slso be attended. Such an arrangement may be good for the student, bat eems to be also designed with an eye to " results," the unsatisfactory and inefficient standard by which the work of the schools will be judged. Nevertheless a large number of candidates remain nocatered for.

There are those persons who have been engaged in business for some years and find a need for some special theoretical or technical instruction to assist them in their daily work ; also those students who having specialised in a particular branch and passed through the prescribed courses. are competent and desire facilities to continue their work on the lines of original research.

A concerti, example may illustrate the position regarding practical students (with limited tune at their dispossl) requiring technical Matt tactic° in one subject. &me years ago • class was provided in \Carrington in Iron and Steel Manufacture, al o in Metallurgy, both very necessary subjects in such an important iron and steel centre. Time classes were abandoned— apparently through lack of students. Further investigation, however, would indicate that the classes were—on the one hand —victim' to the course system as prospective students were either unable or unwilling to take up the other subjects forming the course, and on the other hand to the over economical action of the authority in the matter of equipment. . . . . . . .

. . Then there is the case of the stadent who having completed the set courses in his particular line and possessing initiative desires to continue his studies in the way of original research. He finds that, possess- ing no further value to the school from tho pint of view of" results "no facilities are afforded him and he is comyelled to go out of town with considerable loss of time and with additional expense, otherwise he must give up the work unless he is sufficiently fcrtu. nate to have opportunities afforded for such work from other sources.

In any case it is evident that the present course system does not adequately provide for a large num- ber of deeil able students in more than one branch of industry and while showing good results on paper, is unable to give to the practical student the amount of assistance and encouragement which under somewhat modified conditions would be available.

I may be wrong in the view I take of the main purpose of the Technical Institute, but have always imagined the yrovision of facilities for maintain- ing efficiency in the staple industries of the town to be of greater importance than running the concern with a view to grants and the results of examinations.— Yews &c.,

===== 0002601_19111202_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
H. SHERSURN, RotherwcAStockton Heath, Warrington, November 306, 1911.

Sir,—re letter from "a rejected student complain- ing about the Course System at the Technical schools. The fault lies not in the System itself, which every student who has passed the preliminary and first year courses recognises to be the one and only system of any value, but in the ridiculous and utterly absurd way in which it is administered at Warrington. Why should a student be compelled to take a class, which he knows would be absolutely of no use to him, and be nothing but a waste of erne, simply because the principals, who I may say, do not know what a parti- cular trade or profession requires, think he should ? Why shouldn't he, if the evenings on which the classes are held be suitable, take a in, re suitable and useful subject, without being called upon to pay Lis. extra (an absurd fee—in other towns it is less than the course fee) instead of 7s. 6d. No student objects to a course if he be allowed to have a voice in the choosing of it, and not have any kiwi of;elasses thrust upon him, as is done here but not in other towrs.

A PAST STUDENT.

-BISHOPS WHO DO NOT LEAD

Clergyman's Criticism of Church Divisions.

A striking address on "Th. glorious compreben- ' sivenesas of the English Church" was delivered at a ' meeting of the Warrington branch of the English Church Union last evening by the Rev J F Nuthall, of St. Luke's, Southport. He said that since the breach with Rome the Church of England had been definite in . theory but not in practice. Hosing reviewed the later history of the Courch and showed how it was that no many different schools of thought came into existence, be said there wan no doubt about the fact of the ' Church's comprehecaiveness, but it was another ques- tion as to whether it was glorious. If they looked at Ito evils, they complained that the Bishops did not lend. How could they unless they were men of exceptionally strong character, sthen there were so many sections in the Church to please? The Church of England spoke with no certain voice in her parishes. What was taught in one parish was contiadicted in another. He contended that as means of communict- tion improved and the natives of non-Caristian lands learned more, the differences of teaching emanating from the same Csurch must be a stumbling block to mission work. The prepent " comprehensiveness" lent itself to non-natural interpretation of doctriee. They were not oven agreed on the Apostles Creed. " The Holy Catholic Church " for centuries had had a certain clear and definite meaning. To-day some would include Dissenting bodies in the drfini Lion ant was it therefore surprising if a clergyman who avowed his earnest belief in the Resurrection in the creed also interpreted it in a modern way ? Advising members of the English Church Union as to the course of action they must take the speaker said they must try to convert the Church of England as a whole. There most be no tampering. with the Prayer Book, as revision at the present yoacture would stereotype existing divisions. They must not have a policy of isolation. They had everything to gain by mixing with their brother Churchmen. The. must remember that every parish church in theory was Catholic though in actual practice it did not always come up to that ideal. They must also study the Catholic faith no that they could influence others. Tbe local hon. secretary (Rev. J. H. G. Hulling. worth) reported Ha' the membership of the branch was 4R—an increase of ten cloning the year. Dealing with the present outlook in the Church, he said the Prayer Book might certainly be improved but revision was not desirable at the present time. Tbe book as it stood was one of the principal bonds of uoion in the Chords. Neither of the two bodies—Convocation of the clergy and the Parliament of the nation—to which the work of revision and of legalising each revision would be entrusted could at present, with any propriety, undertake the work. The report also strongly pro(ested against the proposed disendowment of the Church in Wales.

CANON BLOGG'S TRAGIC DEATH. Painful Close of Suffrage Besting.

The Rev. Canon H. B. Blogg, Vicar of Frodsham and Rural Dean, died with tragic suddenness on Wednesday evening on the platform of I rodsham Drill liall. A meeting was being held in support of the aims f the National Union of Women's Suffrage Societies and towards the close Canon Blogg rose to propc,e a vote of thanks to Lady Frances Balfour, the principal speaker. He appeared to be in his usual buoyant quilts and wittily referied to man's infeniority to wLmen. his voice being heard with perfect clearness in all parts of the room. He caused some amusement by saying that woman wanted the vote and his opinion was that if a woman wanted anything, she had better have it. (Laughter and applause). If she didn't she took it. (Laughter.) Everybody knew the " taking' ways of women. (Laughter.) Ile held it right that every woman who was a widow or a spinster and the owner of property should have a vote. That was as far as he had got at present. (Laughter). . . . . . .

Aaer Mentioning that he had purposely taken a seat near the end of the platform in the event of there being a scrimmage that night—(laughter)—Canon Blogg expressed his interest in lady Balfour s speech and said it was his duty to move a vote of thanks, and he did it with much pleasure. Then, without any warning, be collapsed and fell sideways into a chair. Dr. Williams and Dr. Selbv rushed to him, but it was at once seen that his condi- tion was grave. At the request of ILA Itatlibone, who presided, the majority of the audience quickly and noiselessly dispersed. Mrs. Blogg, who was in the ball, was with her husband immediately after the seizure. Restoratives were applied by the doctors but the Vicar showed no signs of returning to conscious- ness. Prayer was offered by the Rev. M. M. Crane. vicar of Alvanley, all on the platform kneeling. At ten o'clock Canon Blogg bieathed his last. The news of the Vicar's tragic end created a profound sensation in the Wage where he wee held, in high regard by all. Cauon 'Hogg, who was s'xty-six years of age. was the son of an officer in the Indian army and was born at Calcutta. Having been educated at Oxford he was ordained to the priesthood iu 1875 and, after holding curacies at Castle Riding and St. Peter-le-Bailey Oxford, and the chaplaincy of Christ Church, Oxford be became Vicar of Frod-Ibm in 1879. During his 1 relic the church was completely restored and the schools brought up-to-date. Canon Blegg was a bold preacher, and some years age caused some stir by advocating Sunday opt it, Ile slid that under certain conditions, such as attendance at church, to Abell. golf and cricket were not only permissible but desirable on Sunday. He was a convinced advocate of compulsory military training and hod on several occasions addressed National Service League meetings at Warriegton.

Applications must be made in candidate's own hand writing on forms to be obtained from this office.
